## 1. Background information on the procedure

## 1.1. Submission of the dossier

The applicant 4Sc AG submitted on 9 February 2024 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Kinselby, through the centralised procedure and falling within the Article 3(1) and point 3 of Annex of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 15 December 2022.

Kinselby, was designated as an orphan medicinal product EU/3/23/2835 on 13 October 2023 in the following condition: Treatment of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (CTCL).

The applicant applied for the following indication: treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS) that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy or total skin electron beam therapy (TSEB).

## 1.2. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application.

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, non-clinical and clinical data based on applicants' own tests and studies and/or bibliographic literature substituting/supporting certain test(s) or study(ies).

## 1.3. Information on Paediatric requirements

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included an EMA Decision P/0376/2017 on the granting of a product-specific waiver for all subsets of the paediatric population.

## 1.4. Information relating to orphan market exclusivity

## 1.4.1. Similarity

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the applicant did submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products.

## 1.5. Applicant's request for consideration

## 1.5.1. New active Substance status

The applicant requested the active substance resminostat contained in the above medicinal product to be considered as a new active substance, as the applicant claims that it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 1.6. Scientific Advice

The applicant received the following scientific advice on the development relevant for the indication subject to the present application:

| 28 January 2016  | EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016  | Hans Ovelgönne and Paolo Foggi   |
| 14 December 2017 | EMA/CHMP/SAWP/796264/2017 | Martin Mengel and Markku Pasanen |
| 25 March 2021    | EMA/SA/0000050948         | Jan Sjoberg and Ole Weis Bjerrum |

The scientific advice pertained to the following quality, non-clinical, and clinical aspects:

-  The definition of the starting materials, the level of impurity in the drug substance and commercial scale;
-  The proposed non-clinical program for marketing authorisation;
-  The design of the phase 2 study 4SC-201-6-2015, in particular the proposed study population, the use and definition of PFS as primary endpoint, the proposed approach for assessing response based on the global response score for Mycosis fungoides / Sézary syndrome, the secondary endpoints and Quality of Life assessments, the proposed cardiac safety monitoring strategy, the statistical assumptions and analysis method.
-  The proposed protocol change from event-triggered to time-point triggered, the planned reduction of the study population size, the introduction of simulations as statistical tool to compensate for missing patients and missing events.

## 2. Scientific discussion

## 2.1. Problem statement

## 2.1.1. Disease or condition

Kinselby is intended for the treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS) that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy or total skin electron beam therapy (TSEB).

## 2.1.2. Epidemiology

Cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (CTCL) is a rare and heterogeneous subgroup of NHL, comprising 2% of lymphomas and approximately 83% of primary cutaneous lymphomas. The most common subtype of CTCL is MF, accounting for 62% of primary CTCL, whereas SS, an erythrodermic and leukemic CTCL variant, occurs less frequently and accounts for 3% of primary CTCL.

The incidence of CTCL has shown a 3-fold increase since the 1970s and appears to have stabilized in the last decade. In North America, the annual overall incidence of CTCL rates vary from 7.7 per million persons from 2001 to 2005 in the United States (US) [Korvgavkar et al., 2013] to 11.32 cases per million individuals per year from 1998 to 2010 in Canada [Ghazawi et al., 2017]. The overall incidence rate for CTCL in Europe was estimated to be 0.29 - 0.39 per 100,000 person-years [Dobos et al., 2020, Saunes et al., 2009].

Data from the Public Health England report in 2016 showed an overall incidence of MF of 3.7 per million persons per year in the United Kingdom (UK) [Kaufmann et al., 2020], which is comparable to the incidence data from the Netherlands (0.49 per 100,000 persons in 2019). In Finland, the prevalence of diagnosed MF rose from 2.04/100,000 to 5.38/100,000 from 1998 through 2016.

The incidence of SS in Europe is considerably lower, at about 0.075 per 100,000 persons with a prevalence of 0.36 per 100,000 [Ottervanger et al., 2021, Keto et al., 2021]. Only limited data are available from Asia, where the incidence of MF was reported to be 0.17 - 0.24 per 100,000 person-years and the prevalence was 0.39 - 0.72 per 100,000 person-years [Park et al., 2022].

## 2.1.3. Biologic features, aetiology and pathogenesis

An heterogeneous spectrum of genetic abnormalities have been identified in CTCL cells, mostly deletions and translocations that involve different chromosomes or chromosomal segments. One report identified dozens of driver mutations, including some that are predicted to affect chromatin; immune surveillance; RNA splicing; and signaling through MAPK, NFkB, PI3K, and T cell receptor. Gene expression profiles and microRNA profiling suggest that MF and SS may be separate entities with differing pathogenesis. Whole-exome sequencing analysis on samples from MF and SS found that, compared with normal controls, the tumoursuppressors MLL3 (KMT2C) and TP53 were the most frequently mutated genes. Point mutations and copy number gains of TNFRSF1B (which encodes tumour necrosis factor receptor 2 [TNFR2]), which is expressed in mature T cells and regulates signaling pathways related to T cell survival and proliferation, were also found with high frequency.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The neoplastic cells in MF and SS are thought to derive from skin tropic memory CD4+ T cells. Based on the studies demonstrating different T cell molecular profiles in MF and SS, it has been hypothesized that MF and SS originate from distinct memory T cell subsets. Malignant T cells from MF skin express the CCR4+/CLA+/CCR7-/L-selectin- immunophenotype of the skin resident memory T cells (TRM). In contrast, clonal malignant T cells from the blood of SS patients express the CCR4+/CCR7+/L-selectin+ phenotype of the skin-tropic central memory T cell (TCM). The expression of surface ligand CCR4 determines the T cell skin homing ability, while CCR7/L-selectin determine their ability to re-circulate between blood and lymph node.

The tumour microenvironment plays a role in the pathogenesis and progression of MF and SS. While in early stage MF, the T cell infiltrate is composed of both malignant CD4+ and reactive CD8+ T cells, with a dominant Th1 cytokine pattern; in advanced lesions and in Sezary syndrome, there is gradual increase in CD4+ T cells and a switch to a Th2-dominated microenvironment. This suggests a loss of CD8-mediated immunity and anti-tumour response with disease progression. The increased production Th2 cytokines interleukin (IL)-4, IL-5, and IL-13 is concurrent with a decrease in the expression of Th1 factors such as T cell-specific T-box transcription factor (T-bet), interferon gamma (IFN-γ), Stat4, and IL-12.

The cause of MF is unclear, but T cell receptor (TCR)/T cell activation, altered JAK-STAT signalling, RNA splicing abnormalities, and epigenetic alterations are common features.

The cause of most cases of SS remains unclear. Tumour cells in SS appear to be derived from skin-homing CD4-positive (CD4+) T cells or central memory T cells. Expression of cutaneous lymphocyte antigen (CLA) and high levels of the chemokine receptors CCR4 and CCR7 provide the T cells with the capacity to traffic bidirectionally into and out of the skin in response to the appropriate chemokines.

Impaired immunity in patients with SS has been attributed, at least partially, to the increased production of Th2 cytokines by the malignant clone; these cytokines antagonize Th1 immunity and lead to decreased production of interleukin (IL)-12 and interferon (IFN) gamma, which are critical for anti-tumour immunity. Activated T cells of patients with SS may also produce increased IL-10 and transforming growth factor (TGF)- beta, which can further impair cell-mediated immunity. In some cases, the malignant T cells can behave like regulatory T cells by manifesting FOXP3, increased CTLA4 expression, and enhanced production of IL-10 or TGF-beta, leading to suppression of normal T cell function. Increased production of IL-31 by the malignant T cells has been associated with worsening pruritus among patients with SS. Abnormalities of immune checkpoint molecules also contribute to depressed Th1 immunity and impaired cell-mediated immunity.

Defects of proapoptotic pathways have been identified, including decreased expression of Fas (CD95), which may be due to hypermethylation of the FAS promoter. Other apoptotic defects include increased expression of the apoptosis inhibitor cFLIP and tumour cell loss of TRAIL-receptor 2, leading to resistance to TRAIL.

Molecular abnormalities among individuals with SS/MF are heterogeneous. However, the pathophysiology appears to involve an intricate network of aberrant signaling through the T cell receptor, NFkB, CCR4/MAPK, and JAK/STAT pathways; aberrant tumour microenvironment, altered cell migration; and abnormal DNA damage response and chromatin structure. Upregulation of GATA3 and decreased expression of STAT4 transcription factors may account for the Th2 phenotype in many cases of SS. TP53 alterations appear to be the most common molecular abnormality in SS, but mutations and/or deletions in ZEB1, RB1, PTEN, DNMT3A, and CDKN1B and gain-of-function mutations in the JAK-STAT pathway are often found.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.1.4. Clinical presentation, diagnosis and stage/prognosis

MF and SS are typically diagnosed in adults with a median age of 55 - 60 years; the prevalence of these conditions is higher among men than in women (approximately 1.6:1, respectively).

For clinical staging of MF and SS, the revised tumour, node, metastasis and blood (TNMB) staging system should be used (Tables below). Apart from clinical stage, older age, large cell transformation and increased LDH values have been identified as independent unfavourable prognostic factors in MF.

MF is typically slow growing and often remains localized to the skin. In early stages of the disease (Stage I and IIA), MF is localized to the skin where it presents clinically as skin discomfort, patches and/or infiltrating plaques. In advanced stages of the disease (Stage IIB, III and IV), lymph nodes and visceral organs may become involved.

SS is defined by the presence of T4 erythroderma (ie, involvement of ≥80 percent body surface area) plus B2 involvement of peripheral blood (ie, ≥1000 Sézary cells/microL), and is staged based on the presence of nodal and/or visceral involvement, as follows:

Stage IVA1 - No significant lymph node or visceral involvement

Stage IVA2 - Lymph node involvement, but no visceral involvement

Stage IVB - Visceral involvement, with or without nodal involvement

SS is staged as T4 N2/3/x M0 B2.

Blood staging for MF/SS is further defined as B0 = &lt;250/ml of CD4+/CD26 or CD4+/CD7 cells, B1 = does not meet criteria for B0 or B2, and B2 ≥1000/ ml of CD4+/CD26 or CD4+/CD7 cells or other aberrant population of lymphocytes identified by flow cytometry [3,240].

Pruritus is the most common symptomatic complaint in patients with MF and may cause severe distress. Ulceration, with secondary infection of the tumours, is a common cause of morbidity [Dummer et al., 2021]. SS is a more aggressive disease and is characterized by the triad of erythroderma, generalized lymphadenopathy, and the presence of neoplastic T-cells (Sézary cells) in the skin, lymph nodes and peripheral blood [Willemze et al., 2019]. SS tends to progress more rapidly than MF and has a worse prognosis [Dummer et al., 2021]. Patients present with generalized erythroderma with exfoliation, edema and lichenification (often &gt; 80% of body surface area). Lymphadenopathy and pruritus are common, alopecia, ectropium, mild palmoplantar keratoderma, and nail onychodystrophy may be present. Patients often shiver and complain of chills and general fatigue [Jawed et al., 2014].

Advanced-stage MF and SS are associated with a major symptom burden, including pruritus (the most prevalent and disturbing symptom), inability to sleep, cutaneous dysesthesia, pain, and disfigurement [Sampogna et al., 2009, Wright et al., 2013]. These symptoms may cause severe distress and adversely affect patients' quality of life (QoL) [Wilcox, 2014].

For patients with advanced stage disease (Stage IIB, III and IVA), the median survival has been estimated to be approximately 2 -5 years; for those with visceral involvement (Stage IVB), the median survival is less than 2 years [Agar et al., 2010]. A recent meta-analysis of stage-specific overall survivance (OS), which included 6,279 patients with MF and SS, demonstrated a significant difference in the OS for Stage IB versus Stage IIB-IVB disease with a strong increase of the pooled hazard ratio (HR) from Stage IIB (HR 7.24; 95%

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

confidence interval (CI) = 5.91, 8.86) to Stage IVB (HR 39.21; CI = 19.73, 77.93). The pooled 5-year calculated OS rates ranged from 62.2% (best 67.9%, worst 56.0%) in Stage IIB to 23.3% (best 39.7%, worst 10%) in Stage IVB [Mourad &amp; Gniadecki, 2020]. The same analysis showed that the development of skin tumours (Stage IIB), SS (Stage IVA1), nodal effacement (Stage IVA2), and metastatic spread (Stage IVB) had a powerful negative impact on survival [Mourad &amp; Gniadecki, 2020].

## 2.1.5. Management

In addition to a correct diagnosis, appropriate staging is fundamental to choose the best therapeutic approach for each individual patient. It is of importance to keep in mind that, although appropriate treatment will be effective in most patients, therapy in MF/SS is generally palliative and should follow a step wise, stage-adapted approach giving priority to maintenance of quality of life.

Patients with early-stage disease (Stages IA, IB and IIA) are generally treated with skin-directed therapies (SDTs) such as topical steroids, psoralen photochemotherapy (psoralen + ultraviolet A light (UVA), PUVA), narrow band ultraviolet B light (UVB), localized and whole skin radiotherapy, and topical cytostatic agents such as chlormethine gel (mechlorethamine).

Systemic therapies are recommended in early-stage disease refractory to SDT and in advanced stages of MF and SS. Response rates and durations are typically short and time to next treatment may provide a useful surrogacy of real-life drug benefit.

In patients developing few infiltrated plaques of tumours (Stage IIB) low-dose local radiotherapy is recommended. Systemic treatment is indicated in patients with more infiltrated plaques and tumours; interferon-α (IFN-α) or retinoids (bexarotene) commonly combined with PUVA or other SDTs, or a combination of IFN-α and retinoids, or total skin electron beam (TSEB) therapy should be considered. For patients with SS or erythrodermic MF (i.e., leukemic forms of CTCL), European Society for Medical Oncology (ESMO) guidelines suggest extracorporeal photopheresis (ECP), either alone or in combination with other skin-directed or systemic therapies (Willemze et al 2018). Targeted therapies such as brentuximab vedotin (CD30 antibody) and mogamulizumab (CCR4 antibody) are approved for patients with relapsed or refractory CTCL, i.e., after at least one prior systemic therapy. Other agents such as the fusion toxin denileukin diftitox and the histone deacetylase (HDAC) inhibitors vorinostat and romidepsin have been approved in the US by the FDA for patients with relapsed and refractory CTCL but have not been approved for CTCL in Europe.

Treatment for SS is similar to that for advanced MF, with some distinctive exceptions, including the use of extracorporeal photopheresis (ECP) and low dose alemtuzumab, and the need for adjuvant treatment to control pruritus.

For refractory SS patients, alemtuzumab (off-label), systemic chemotherapy and allogeneic stem cell transplantation (alloSCT) are recommended. AlloSCT has been associated with treatment-free long-term remissions and holds a potential for cure in this disease in highly selected, younger patients, but is associated with considerable morbidity and non-relapse mortality. There remains therefore a need for additional treatment options.

## 2.2. About the product

Resminostat is an oral histone deacetylase (HDAC) inhibitor with particular activity against HDAC6. It is selective for class I, IIb and IV HDAC enzymes, with pronounced activity against HDAC6, and dosedependently induces acetylation of histone and non-histone proteins. HDACs are enzymes that catalyze the removal of acetyl groups from specific histone sites, in particular at promotor and enhancer regions of the DNA, which is an essential part of the regulation of cellular gene transcription. HDACs also regulate gene expression in an indirect fashion by mediating the acetylation of non-histone proteins such as DNA-binding proteins, transcription factors, signal transducers, and DNA repair and chaperone proteins. Histone modification is one of three major mechanisms of epigenetic modification which alters DNA accessibility, chromatin structure, and, subsequently, regulation of gene expression patterns, with significant relevance in a wide range of physiological and pathophysiological processes [Bondarev et al., 2021]. HDAC expression and activity are frequently increased in tumours, indicating a therapeutic potential of HDAC inhibitors in various hematological and solid malignancies [Li &amp; Zhu 2014, Gryder et al., 2012, Ellis &amp; Pili 2010].

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The proposed indication for resminostat is treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS) that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy or Total Skin Electron Beam therapy (TSEB).

The recommended dose of resminostat is 600 mg per day taken orally once daily. This corresponds to 3 tablets of 200 mg resminostat. Treatment is to be administered on Days 1 - 5 of a 14-day treatment cycle; the 5 days of treatment are followed by a 9-day treatment-free period. Treatment continues until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

## 2.3. Type of Application and aspects on development

This in an application for a standard MA.

## 2.4. Quality aspects

## 2.4.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as film-coated tablets containing 200 mg of resminostat. The product contains the salt resminostat mesilate.

Other ingredients are:

Tablet core: Lactose monohydrate, microcrystalline cellulose, povidone, crospovidone, magnesium stearate.

Film coating: Lactose monohydrate, hypromellose (E464), titanium dioxide (E171), triacetin, iron oxide (yellow) (E172).

The product was proposed to be available in blisters of PVC/PE/PVdC (thermoforming foil) and aluminium (heat sealing foil).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.4.2. Active Substance

## 2.4.2.1. General information

The chemical name of resminostat mesilate is (2E)-3-[1({4-[(dimethylamino)methyl]phenyl}sulfonyl)-1Hpyrrol-3-yl]-N-hydroxyprop-2-enamide methanesulfonate corresponding to the molecular formula C17H23N3O7S2. It has a relative molecular mass of 445.51 g/mol and the following structure:

Figure 1: active substance structure

<!-- image -->

The chemical structure of resminostat was elucidated by a combination of elemental analysis, 1 H-NMR, 13 CNMR, IR, MS, UV spectroscopy. The solid state properties of the active substance were measured by XRPD, TGA, DSC.

The active substance is a white-off-white to light brown powder, it exhibits pH dependent aqueous solubility, in the pH range of 1.2 - 4.5 the active substance is highly soluble while at pH 6.8 the solubility is low. It is non-hygroscopic.

Resminostat mesilate has a non-chiral molecular structure and polymorphism has not been observed.

## 2.4.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation and process controls

Resminostat mesilate is synthesized in five main steps using well defined starting materials with acceptable specifications. There is one optional recrystallisation step for one of the starting materials before commencing the synthetic process, which is referred to as step 0.

The description of the resminostat mesilate manufacturing process is acceptable, and the operating parameters and in-process controls are suitably presented and justified. The specifications and control methods for intermediate products, starting materials and reagents have been presented. One of the initially proposed starting materials RESI-05 was not considered acceptable and the applicant was requested to redefine the starting material to a precursor used in the synthesis, 4-BrTosCl. A major objection (MO) was raised on this aspect; it was, thus appropriate for this material (4-BrTosCl) to be regarded as the starting material. The applicant redefined the starting material in line with the request and the MO was resolved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on chemistry of new active substances. Potential and actual impurities were well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised. The product is intended for the treatment of a condition within the scope of the ICH S9 guideline and this is accounted for in the evaluation of potential impurities.

The commercial manufacturing process for the active substance was developed in parallel with the clinical development program. The quality of the active substance used in the various phases of the development is considered to be comparable with that produced by the proposed commercial process.

The active substance is packaged in LDPE bags which are placed into HDPE drums the primary packaging complies with Commission Regulation (EU) 10/2011, as amended.

## 2.4.2.3. Specification

The active substance specification includes tests for: appearance (visual), identity (IR, HPLC), assay (HPLC, ion-chromatography), impurities (HPLC), residual solvents (GC), water content (KF), sulfated ash (Ph. Eur.), particle size (laser light diffraction), microbiological quality (Ph. Eur.).

The active substance specification parameters and limits are in line with relevant guidelines and are acceptable. Impurities present at higher than the qualification threshold according to ICH Q3A were qualified by toxicological and clinical studies and appropriate specifications have been set. The impurity RESI-07 is a primary metabolite of the product and the limit is toxicologically appropriate.

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and non-compendial methods appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing has been presented.

Batch analysis data of nine commercial scale batches of the active substance from the proposed commercial manufacturing site are provided. The results are within the specifications and consistent from batch to batch.

## 2.4.2.4. Stability

Stability data from six commercial batches of active substance from the proposed manufacturer stored in a container closure system representative of that intended for the market for up to 60 months under long term conditions (25 ºC / 60% RH) and for up to six months under accelerated &amp; intermediate conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH &amp; 30 ºC / 65% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided.

The parameters tested are the same as for release. At long term and accelerated conditions all tested parameters were within the specifications and no trends were observed.

Photostability testing following the ICH guideline Q1B was performed on one batch. Results on stress conditions of acidic, basic, aqueous, oxidative and increased temperatures were also provide on one batch.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The photostability testing demonstrated the active substance is sensitive to light. During the forced degradation studies the active substance was relatively stable when exposed to aqueous, acidic conditions, and when exposed to elevated temperatures. Degradation was however observed upon exposure to basic and oxidative conditions.

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed supplier is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 60 months at the applicant's selected storage condition of 15-25°C and protected from light in the proposed container.

## 2.4.3. Finished Medicinal Product

## 2.4.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

The finished product is a 200 mg film-coated tablet, it is yellow, approximately 5.5 × 10.2 mm, biconvex, round, with a bevelled edge.

The aim of development was to create a suitable immediate release oral dosage form for the intended oncology based indication. A quality target product profile (QTTP) was developed on this basis

Physical characteristics of the active substance which could impact the performance of the finished product are appropriately justified and controlled. The active substance demonstrates pH dependant solubility, and particle size limits for the active substance have been defined in the specification based on the development performed. No polymorphism of the active substance is reported and in addition it is non-hygroscopic.

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation. Functionality related characteristics are included for relevant excipients to ensure appropriate control.

The formulation for the product was developed in conjunction with the clinical programme, in early studies 100 mg and 200 mg gelatin capsules were used for administration to patients. Following this development of a tablet formulation was initiated. Initially two strengths of tablets were to be pursued, with 100 mg and 200 mg strength tablet formulations, however development of the 100 mg strength was discontinued. The development of the 200 mg strength aimed to identify a formulation with sufficient mechanical strength and stability, and a number of potential formulations were trialled. These investigations led to the final formulation which was named as formulation 4, this formulation is the same as was used in the final pivotal clinical study.

The information provided with respect to product dissolution and the selected method was initially not considered acceptable. An MO was raised which encompassed a number of dissolution related aspects, and as part of this the applicant was requested to select a dissolution limit which was in line with the performance of the batches used in the clinical studies. The applicant was also requested to outline the potential impact of the particle size of the active substance on the dissolution performance, and to provide data demonstrating that the dissolution profiles of proposed commercial batches were similar to the batches used in the clinical studies. In response to the MO the applicant demonstrated that the particle size of the active substance within the ranges that are manufactured do not impact the performance of the finished product. The applicant also developed a new dissolution method aimed at reducing variability in early time-points which was considered acceptable and the applicant demonstrated that the dissolution performance of the clinical and commerical batches was similar. Following a further request the applicant also tightened the proposed dissolution limit proposed for use with the revised method to align with the dissolution performance of the clinical batches and the MO was resolved. Discriminatory power of the revised dissolution method was demonstrated as differences in profiles were observed with tablets of varying harndess.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The primary packaging is blisters of PVC/PE/PVdC (thermoforming foil) and aluminium (heat sealing foil). Where relevant the material complies with Ph. Eur. and EC requirements. The choice of the container closure system has been validated by stability data and is adequate for the intended use of the product.

## 2.4.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

The finished product is manufactured at one manufacturing site and satisfactory information concerning GMP has been provided. The manufacturing process consists of eight main steps including dry blending, wet granulation, drying, main blending, final blending, compression, coating and packaging. The process is considered to be a standard process.

The steps that can have a significant impact on the processability of the intermediates and on the resulting properties of the finished product have been identified as critical process steps. These steps are controlled by the respective normal operating range (NOR) and proven acceptable range (PAR) and adequate in-process controls.

The manufacturing process is standard, and major steps of the manufacturing process have been validated by a number of studies. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner. Information on potential bulk hold times are presented and appropriately supported by stability data.

## 2.4.3.3. Product specification

The finished product release specifications includes appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form; description (visual), size (caliper), uniformity of mass (Ph. Eur.), disintegration (Ph. Eur.), uniformity of dosage units (Ph. Eur.), dissolution (HPLC), identification (IR, HPLC), assay (HPLC), impurities (HPLC), microbiological quality (Ph. Eur.).

The specification for the control of the finished product contains the typical tests for this type of pharmaceutical form and the limits have been adequately justified.

The limit for one specified impurity (RESI-07) exceeds the ICH Q3B qualification threshold, the proposed limit for this impurity is considered justified on the basis of toxicological data outlining that this impurity is a primary metabolite of the active substance. The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product has been assessed following a risk-based approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Based on the risk assessment it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls. The information on the control of elemental impurities is satisfactory.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The product is within the scope of ICH S9 which is relevant to the assessment of potential mutagenic impurities such as nitrosamines. In the context of ICH S9 the potential limits for these impurities would be set in accordance with ICH Q3B. A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities along with supportive testing data for the potential presence of NDMA and NMBA was provided. The risk assessment and testing data demonstrate that the relevant limits of ICH Q3B will not be exceeded. Considering the ICH S9 indication and the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing has been presented.

Batch analysis results have been provided for batches manufactured across the clinical program including two commercial scale batches and two pilot scale batches produced at the proposed manufacturing site. The results confirm the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

## 2.4.3.4. Stability of the product

Stability data from two commercial and one pilot scale batch of finished product stored for up to 60 months under long term conditions (25 ºC / 60% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. The batches of medicinal product are representative of those proposed for marketing and were packed in the intended primary packaging.

Samples were tested for appearance, disintegration (Ph. Eur.), dissolution (HPLC), assay (HPLC), impurities (HPLC), and microbiological quality (Ph. Eur.). The analytical procedures used are stability indicating. The long term and accelerated data shows very little change over time and all results remain within the specifications.

In addition, one batch was exposed to light as defined in the ICH Guideline on Photostability Testing of New Drug Substances and Products. The finished product is not sensitive to light.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 5 years without any special storage conditions are acceptable.

## 2.4.3.5. Adventitious agents

It is confirmed that the lactose is produced from milk from healthy animals in the same condition as those used to collect milk for human consumption and that the lactose has been prepared without the use of ruminant material other than calf rennet according to the Note for Guidance on Minimising the Risk of Transmitting Animal Spongiform Encephalopathy Agents Via Human and veterinary medicinal products.

## 2.4.4. Discussion on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the course of the procedure two major objections concerning quality aspects were raised, these concerned the definition of one of the starting materials used for the manufacture of the active substance and the development and limit selected for the dissolution method proposed for the control of the finished product. To resolve these objections the applicant redefined the starting material as requested and developed a new dissolution method and demonstrated that the limit proposed using this method provided suitable assurance for quality control of the finished product.

The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

At the time of the CHMP opinion, there was one minor unresolved quality issue having no impact on the Benefit/Risk ratio of the product,. This is put forward and agreed as recommendations for future quality development.

## 2.4.5. Conclusions on the chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

The quality of this product is considered to be acceptable when used in accordance with the conditions defined in the proposed SmPC. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way. Data has been presented to give reassurance on TSE safety.

## 2.4.6. Recommendation(s) for future quality development

In the context of the obligation of the MAHs to take due account of technical and scientific progress, the CHMP recommends the following points for investigation:

1. The applicant is recommended to update the manufacturing process of one of the starting materials (4BrTosCl). Timeline; prior to the next manufacture of an active substance batch which is expected to take place in 2026.

## 2.5. Non-clinical aspects

## 2.5.1. Introduction

Resminostat is an oral histone deacetylase (HDAC) inhibitor 6. Resminostat is selective for class I, IIb and IV HDAC enzymes, with pronounced activity against HDAC6, and dose-dependently induces acetylation of histone and non-histone proteins. The pharmacodynamic activity of resminostat has been characterised in a wide range of in vitro and in vivo studies, demonstrating both its mode of action as an inhibitor of human histone deacetylases and its anticancer activity in murine tumor models. The safety pharmacology of resminostat with respect to CNS, respiration and the cardiovascular system has been scrutinised in a large panel of assays (in agreement with ICH M3(R2)). Pharmacokinetic studies comprised:Plasma protein binding in various species, Metabolite profiling in hepatocytes from various species, metabolism in human liver microsomes and CYP enzymes, inhibition of CYP enzymes, [14C]-Pharmacokinetics, [14C]-Tissue distribution and metabolite profiling and a comprehensive in vitro study to assess the drug-drug interaction potential of resminostat. The nonclinical toxicology program is developed in line with the ICH S9 Guideline and the corresponding Q&amp;A document (2018).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.5.2. Pharmacology

## 2.5.2.1. Primary pharmacodynamic studies

Resminostat inhibits class I, IIb and IV HDAC enzymes (HDAC1-11) in vitro with IC50 in a nanomolar range. The most pronounced effect is on HDAC6 with IC50 of 10 nM in biochemical assays. The resminostat metabolites M7, M10, M12 and M16 did not show significant inhibitory activity against the tested HDACs. In addition, HDAC inhibition, acetylation of α-tubulin and histone H3 were established.

The antiproliferative activity was further confirmed in the CTCL cell lines My-La CD4+ (Mycosis fungoides), HH (Mycosis fungoides) and HuT78 (Sézary syndrome), and it was comparable to vorinostat. The antiproliferative activity was observed in HH cells (EC50 0.74 µM after 72 h). A broad spectrum of antiproliferative activity against 77 different human cancer cell lines was established, with and GI50 values of 0.05-5.81 μM (mean GI50: 2.03 μM), and the selectivity towards specific tumour entities was not detected. For non-tumour cells PBMCs and human fetal lung myofibroblast line IMR90, IC50 values of 8.7 and 10.9 μM were observed, respectively.

In addition, anti-proliferative effects of resminostat mesilate and resminostat hydrochloride were also tested on panels of 22 and 26 different human cancer cell lines. Differences between those two resminostate salts were determined. Later, both substances were used in different studies in vivo. However, considering that in later developmental studies (including clinical studies) only resminostat mesylate was used, this issue was not pursued further.

Studies on cell cycle distribution revealed that treatment with resminostat increases cells in SubG1 phase (representing cells undergoing apoptosis) in all three cell lines HH, My-La CD4+ and HuT78, and the strongest effect is in HH cells. However, overall, resminostat induces only minor changes in cell cycle distribution. The reversibility of the cell cycle alterations was established in A549 cells.

The activity of resminostat in normal human dermal fibroblast (NHDF) and prostate endothelial cells (PrEC) during density-arrested, non-proliferating conditions was considerably lower. Under proliferating conditions, weak and non-cytotoxic growth inhibitory effects were observed for resminostat.

The provided results from study on the CTCL-associated genes STAT4 and STAT6 in vitro using HH and My-La CD4+ cell lines, showed a dose-dependent increase of STAT4 (Th1-type) expression and a reduced STAT6 (Th2-type) expression in both cell lines after treatment with resminostat. It suggests a reversion of Th2 to Th1 phenotype in CTCL cells by resminostat and support the involvement of other mechanisms in regulation of gene repression by resminostat.

Further studies were performed analysing resminostat's effect on T-cell and NK cell responses to cancer cells. Resminostat reduced the inducible expression of immunosuppressive enzymes IDO1 and ARG1 and the constitutive expression of IDO1. Also, resminostat increased the expression of various TAAs, MHC class I and II molecules and co-stimulatory molecules on tumour cells and upregulated the expression of activation marker and co-stimulatory molecules on pDCs and monocytes/macrophages. This supports the Applicant's suggestion that resminostat might enhance tumour immunogenicity and activate immune cells. Provided in vitro data also suggest that resminostat enhances cell lysis of tumour cells via NKG2D ligand interaction.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The data from cytotoxicity studies of resminostat in combination with rituximab (anti-CD20 antibody), suggest that resminostat may enhance the B-cell lymphoma's sensitivity towards rituximab-mediated cytolysis by NK cells. Another study, done with combination of resminostat and mogamulizumab, showed that HuT78 cells pre-treated with resminostat become more susceptible to NK cell-mediated lysis, in the presence of anti-chemokine receptor 4 (CCR4) opsonizing antibody mogamulizumab. However, considering that KINSELBY is indicated as monotherapy for the treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS), these studies could be considered as supportive.

In vivo studies were conducted in different mouse orthotopic and subcutaneous xenograft tumour models. Resminostat hydrochloride or resminostat mesilate were administered orally (suggested clinical administration route) as suspensions of 4% Methocel or 20% PEG/4% Methocel to nude mice inoculated subcutaneously with tumour cells or PBS (different formulation). The effect of resminostat was evaluated based on treated to control (T/C) value to compare tumour volume changes before and after treatment.

The data are limited only to models of lung, colon, prostate, breast, and liver tumours. No histological or laboratory findings apart from tumour volume change are provided.

Results from different studies were inconsistent. E.g. resminostat at 120 mg in A549 xenograft model in two different studies [327-2006] and [328-2006] showed different in vivo effects in tumour growth. In addition, differences were observed using different resminostat salts in the same mouse model (HCl and mesylate, study [328-2006]). In most in vivo studies, resminostat HCl was used, while the form for authorisation is resminostat mesylate. Considering that further PK and safety studies were performed with resminostat mesylate, this issue was not pursued further.

Noteworthy, relevant data could have been obtained from in vivo cancer models other than CTLC (SS/MF). For example, in vitro data suggested that treatment with resminostat strongly increased the expression of CD56 and CD69 markers reflecting NK cell activation and demonstrated increased cytotoxicity against cancer cells as well as the possible protective role of ADCC mechanism when resminostat was used in combination with opsonizing antibodies; however, no NK-mediated effects of resminostat were assessed in the selected in vivo models to validate the in vitro effects, which is a shortcoming. It is also worth noting that the chosen rodent models (immunodeficient mice lacking either functional T/B cells or T/B/NK cells) inherently cannot validate the obtained in vitro data regarding the immune system-mediated mode of action (MoA) of resminostat (Th1/Th2 balance, T-cell mediated effects).

In all tumour models, repeated oral dosing of resminostat for up to 21 days was well tolerated with mild and transient body weight losses. In the A549 model, treatment with resminostat resulted in dose-related inhibition of tumour growth (15 to 120 mg/kg/dose). However, in an orthotopic xenograft model of human Hep3B2.1-7 hepatic carcinoma, only a trend in dose-dependent inhibition of tumour growth was observed; there was a large variability in the data.

In vivo, anti-tumour activity was observed in various human orthotopic and subcutaneous xenograft mouse tumour models at doses inducing mild to moderate and reversible body weight losses and changes in hematological parameters. Lethality was reported for single animals treated in some of the high-dose cohorts well exceeding anticipated human doses.

## 2.5.2.2. Secondary pharmacodynamic studies

To estimate resminostat target binding parameters such as affinity and selectivity, displacement assays with resminostat hydrochloride were performed using histamine H1 and H2 receptors, rat L-type Ca 2+ -channels

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(dihydropyridine site) and Na + -channels. Only weak inhibition of human TACE was detectable for resminostat hydrochloride with 26% inhibition at 10 μM. From these data, an IC50 value of ≥ 50 μM is estimated. Since it is an inhibitor of HDAC 1 in low nanomolar concentrations (IC50 value of 42.5 nM), it could be agreed that the weak inhibition of TACE with IC50 ≥ 50 μM (&gt; 100-fold above) is of low clinical relevance. At single concentrations of 10 μM resminostat hydrochloride, no significant inhibition of enzymatic activity was detected using human phosphodiesterase 3, human metalloproteinases 1 and 7 and human acetylcholine esterase, and no significant activity on ligand binding using histamine H1 and H2 receptors, rat L-type Ca 2+ - channels (dihydropyridine site) and Na + -channels.

## 2.5.2.3. Safety pharmacology programme

Safety pharmacology studies include analyses of resminostat (mesilate or hydrochloride) effects on central nervous system, respiratory and cardiovascular parameters.

No CNS effects were seen in sexually mature mice and rats following a single oral or intravenous dose at levels up to 30 mg/kg. Segregation and slightly decreased activity were observed both in mice and in rats up to 60 min after administration of 300 mg/kg. In both mice and rats, the dose of 30 mg/kg was considered the NOEL for behavior after oral or i.v. administration of resminostat. Following repeated dosing in mice and dogs (5-day repeat-dose studies in rats and dogs and 4-week and 3-month, repeat-dose studies in mice and dogs), CNS effects were not observed.

No treatment-related respiratory effects were observed in single-dose or repeated-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs.

Noteworthy, the behavioural effects were evaluated exclusively in female rodents while the respiratory function only in males.

In vitro hERG inhibition was evaluated in CHO K1 cells of resminostat hydrochloride at concentrations up to 300 μM. Dose-dependent reduction of the hERG peak tail was detected: 7% (10 μM), 19% (30 μM), 33% (100 μM) and 54% (300 μM), with a calculated IC50 of 245 μM. These data indicate a potential for QT prolongation. The QT prolongation properties are also evident from the in vivo assessment. The hERG patch clamp data indicate that the max concentration of resminostat free base on hERG at therapeutic doses (600 mg) would be 1.494 μM, which is 0.6% of the IC50.

The hemodynamic effects of cumulative infusions of resminostat were analyzed in anesthetized guinea pigs. Effects on cardiovascular parameters were established after single and multiple oral administration of resminostat in conscious telemetered dogs. In both species, slight decreases in blood pressure were reported more frequently at higher doses. The slight to moderate increases in heart rate (early tachycardia) noted after treatment with resminostat were without dose-relationship, reversible and considered of minor toxicological relevance. Transient shortening of QT and QTc intervals was observed early (within 8.5 h) after treatment and transient prolongation of QT was observed late (within 13-22 h) after dosing, partially accompanied by decreased heart rates, but without correlation to plasma levels of resminostat. However, in clinical studies, there were a few clinically significant ECG findings, such as permanent atrial fibrillation or signs of a previous myocardial infarction, though no correlation between ∆QTcF and resminostat plasma concentrations was observed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.5.2.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

The provided information on PD interactions includes the results from in vitro and in vivo combination studies of resminostat and drug substances used in FOLFIRI and FOLFOX chemotherapy regimens (5-FU, SN-38 (the active metabolite of irinotecan), and oxaliplatin, and sorafenib.

In vitro combination studies with 5-FU, SB-38 and oxaliplatin were performed in colon carcinoma cell lines, and combination indices (Cis) were calculated using Chou-Talalay method. The combinations with oxaliplatin at ED50 in all three colon cancer cell lines indicate additive or antagonistic effects, while combinations with 5FU or SN38 show synergistic activity in vitro.

In vivo combination studies with irinotecan or sorafenib were done in colon and liver xenograft mouse models. Resminostat at 140 mg/kg/d (p.o.), irinotecan at 50 mg/kg/week (i.p.) and their combination after 14 days of treatment inhibited HCT116 colon tumour growth significantly. The combination of resminostat and irinotecan at the same concentrations was more effective. The body weight losses were comparable between all treated groups; however, the mortalities were observed only in the combination group. The results from the study in orthotopic xenograft mouse model using Hep3B2.1-7 human liver tumour cells revealed higher effect of resminostat mesilate (15 or 40 mg/kg) and sorafenib (40 mg/kg) combination than monotherapy with either one component once daily for three weeks.

## 2.5.3. Pharmacokinetics

## 2.5.4. Toxicology

Toxicological evaluations aligned with ICH S9 (Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceutical, 2019). The non-clinical toxicity profile of oral resminostat was characterized in non-GLP and GLP-compliant single and repeat-dose toxicity studies (up to 3 months) in mice, rats and dogs. The mouse and dog were the toxicological species selected for pivotal toxicity studies based on suitable pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles and metabolism. In addition, in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies and an in phototoxicity study were conducted. Genotoxicity was evaluated using the standard battery of genotoxicity studies according to ICH Guidance on genotoxicity testing and data interpretation for pharmaceuticals intended for human use [ICH S2.R1]. All pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies and genotoxicity phototoxicity studies were conducted according to GLP. Nonclinical carcinogenicity or developmental and reproductive toxicity were not assessed as this is not required for pharmaceuticals intended for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer according to ICH S9. In addition, no studies on the embryo-fetal development have been conducted. In accordance with ICH S5 Detection of Toxicity to Reproduction for Human Pharmaceuticals (2020) [ICH S5], nonclinical developmental and reproductive toxicity (DART) studies may be waived if warranted by a thorough consideration of the targeted patient population, formulation and route of administration, relevant data on toxicity, pharmacodynamics, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacological similarity (class effects), or aspects of the general biology of the pharmaceutical target. Phototoxicity was tested using the 3T3 Neutral Red Uptake Phototoxicity Test for which an OECD guideline [Test No. 432] was available. This complies with ICH Guideline for photosafety evaluation of pharmaceuticals [ICH S10].

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.5.4.1. Single dose toxicity

Single dose toxicity of oral resminostat was evaluated in rats and dogs using gavage [393-2006, 384- 2006] or capsules [400-2006]. Single doses of 0 (vehicle control), 196, 392 or 784 mg/kg resminostat were administered orally (gavage) to rats [393-2006]. All animals (3 males and 3 females per dose) were sacrificed on Day 15. The dose of 196 mg/kg was considered the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL), since only small and reversible hematological changes were detected at this dose level. Dogs were orally administered single doses of 78.4 or 157 mg/kg resminostat in one study [384-2006]. Only one animal per sex was observed in study 384-2006. Typically, acute toxicity studies involve multiple animals per sex (at least 3) to account for individual variability and ensure a more reliable toxicity evaluation. In another study [400-2006], dogs received single doses of 157 mg/kg resminostat orally via capsules, followed by a 2-week recovery phase.

The administration of single doses of resminostat to dogs by gavage showed dose-dependent effects, with adverse effects such as marked vomiting, slightly decreased activity, slightly softer feces observed, particularly at higher doses. Hematological changes were noted but transient, indicating reversible effects. Resminostat administered orally by capsule to beagle dogs at a single dose of 200 mg/kg also caused vomiting. Administration by capsule did not reduce the emetic effect, but the systemic exposure was lower than the plasma AUCs obtained after administration of the same dose by gavage. The 157 mg/kg dose was determined as the maximum tolerated dose (MTD), supported by AUC0-24h values. Additionally, single-dose studies lacked GLP conditions, though they may be considered appropriate given support from GLP repeateddose toxicity studies.

## 2.5.4.2. Repeat dose toxicity

Non-pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies of oral resminostat were conducted in mice [13-2007, 313-2007, 1186], rats [394-2006, 22-2007] and dogs [449-2006, 21-2007] using oral gavage for administration. The majority of findings following repeated oral dosing of resminostat were observed in the hemolymphopoietic system, the gastrointestinal tract, and the male reproductive organs.

In mice [study 13-2007], resminostat doses ranged from 24 to 141 mg/kg/day, with a NOAEL of 24 mg/kg/day. This study demonstrated hematological, clinical chemistry, and histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic system, particularly at the highest dose (141 mg/kg). However, partial recovery was observed in hematological and histopathological changes. Study [313-2007] investigated all BYK compounds, with compound BYK 408740 (used in further pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies) not showing the highest systemic exposure. The highest exposure was observed with BYK397957, while the lowest was with BYK406490. Non-pivotal studies in mice were not conducted under GLP conditions.

Rats were used in 2 non-pivotal repeat dose studies, with one study [22-2006] conducted under GLP conditions. In rats, resminostat doses ranged from 100 to 471 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL observed in rats was 118 mg/kg/day [394-2006] and 100 mg/kg/day [22-2007]. Mortality was observed at the dose of 471 mg/kg/day [394-2006]. Partially reversible hemolymphopoietic changes were mainly observed with resminostat doses starting from 200 mg/kg/day. Histopathological changes were found in the GI tract and reproductive system.

Dogs were used in 2 non-pivotal repeated dose studies, with one study [21-2007] conducted under GLP conditions. In dogs, resminostat doses ranged from 20 to 80 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL observed in dogs was 20 mg/kg/day, while the MTD ranged from 40 to 80 mg/kg/day in both studies. It is important to note that ECG changes (increased QT and QTc) were observed at resminostat doses starting from 40 mg/kg/day. Mortality was observed at the dose of 80 mg/kg/day in both studies. Partially reversible changes in hematology and clinical biochemistry were observed at lower doses. Doses of 40 and 80 mg/kg showed histopathological changes mainly in the hemolymphopoietic, reproductive, and digestive systems. In nonpivotal studies, rats were found to be less exposed to resminostat following oral administration. Mouse and dog have comparable steady state AUCs based on dose per body surface area, appropriate pharmacokinetic profiles and metabolism. Pivotal repeat dose studies were conducted using two different species (mice and dogs). All four studies were conducted under GLP conditions. According to the Guideline on repeated dose toxicity (18 March 2010 CPMP/SWP/1042/99 Rev 1 Corr*), repeated dose toxicity studies should be carried out in conformity with the provisions relating to good laboratory practice (GLP) laid down by Council Directives 87/18/EEC and 88/320/EEC. Therefore, this requirement is fully met. In mice, resminostat doses ranged from 17.5 to 140 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL in a shorter duration (28-days) study [344-2007] was 35 mg/kg/day, while in the 3-month duration study, the NOAEL was established at 70 mg/kg/day. The MTD in study [344-2007] was indicated as 140 mg/kg/day, and mortality was observed at this dose. The studies showed dose related decreases in hematological parameters at all doses (up to ~60% decrease in leukocytes and neutrophils, up to ~36% decrease in platelets at dose of 70 mg/kg). Decreases in lymphocytes were seen at 70 mg/kg dose in 13 weeks study, but at all doses in the 28-day study. All effects were usually almost reversible during the recovery period. Hematology parameters did not recover only at the highest dose (140 mg/kg). Also, the histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic and reproductive systems were significant only at the highest resminostat dose. Histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic system at all doses included lymphoid depletion of the thymus and lymph nodes, increased severity of extramedullary hemopoiesis of the spleen. Reproductive toxicities in male animals included decreased testicular weights, germ cell depletion and decreased spermatozoa in the testes and epididymides at a dose of 140 mg/kg/day. These changes were not completely reversible after the recovery period (germ cell depletion, spermatozoa reduction, intratubular degenerated germ cells were reported). In female mice, changes in the ovaries (follicular atrophy), were detected in all dose groups, and atrophy of the uterus and vagina, reduced (relative and absolute) weight of the ovaries were detected at 140 mg/kg/day. After the recovery period, only animals in the 140 mg/kg/day group still showed findings in the ovaries; in all other groups these changes were completely reversible within 14 days. No findings in the reproductive organs in male and female animals and no histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic system are detected in the 13 weeks study in mice. In dogs, resminostat doses varied from 10 to 40 mg/kg/day across two separate studies. When considering the toxicity objectives and severity, the effects of repeated resminostat doses at 10 and 20 mg/kg/day showed similar outcomes between the 28-Day and 3-month studies. In the 28-days study increased toxicity manifested as vomiting, decreased activity, decreased food intake and weight loss, decreased muscle tone and/or tremor were observed at the high dose 40 mg/kg/day. The doses of 10 mg and 20 mg kg/day were well tolerated. Reduced leukocytes (20-70% at the end of the treatment period), red blood cells (0 to 35% lower at the end of the treatment period), reticulocytes (65-100% on Day 15) and thrombocytes were observed at all dose levels. These changes were reversible during the recovery period. Dose-related changes in clinical chemistry parameters such as increases in total bilirubin (up to 4-fold), decreases in AST (up to 10%), decreases in ALT (up to 45%) and decreases in electrolytes (up to 15% decrease in potassium in females) were observed. In addition, lymphoid depletion in thymus at all doses, in lymph nodes at 20-40

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

mg/kg/day and in spleen at 40 mg/kg/day was observed. Lymphoid depletion in the thymus was still observed after the recovery period.

In all dose groups, germ cell depletion in the testes, oligospermia in the epididymides and atrophy of the prostate were observed in males and changes in the ovaries, uterus, and mammary glands in females. Changes in the male genital tract were seen even after the recovery period.

An increased incidence of duodenal crypt dilatation, with necrotic debris in the lumen, was seen in males at 10 mg/kg/day and in both sexes at the doses of 20 and 40 mg/kg/day. These changes were completely reversible.

No treatment-related changes were observed in ECG, ophthalmoscopy and in coagulation and urinary parameters.

No treatment-related deaths were observed during the 3-month study. Slight signs of systemic toxicity were observed at a dose of 5 mg resminostat/kg b.w./day, including salivation, emesis, a pale gingiva and head tremor, changes in biochemical parameters and a decreased liver weight. Histopathological examination revealed changes in the testes (spermiogenesis disorders, atrophy of the germinal epithelium), epididymides (oligo- /aspermia), prostate (atrophy), liver (increased severity of brownish pigment in Kupffer cells), uterus (inactivation of the uterine mucosa), mammary gland (inactivation) and a slight decrease in the severity of the lymphatic activation (lymphoid hyperplasia) in the lymph nodes and the intestines of males and/or females.

At the dose of 10 mg /kg b.w./day, additional changes were observed in the form of changes in haematological parameters (reduced lymphocytes (up to 40%), leukocytes (up to 60%), erythrocytes (up to 12%), increased reticulocytes (up to 97%)) and reduced testicular and/or prostate weights.

Animals in the 20 mg/kg b.w./day group showed changes in the form of a reduced body weight and pronounced changes in the testes (disturbance of the spermiogenesis (reduced number, viability and motility of spermatids)). At the end of the 12-week recovery period (limited to the high-dose group) most of the changes observed during the treatment period had completely recovered. However, the sperm analysis still showed a slight reduction in sperm count, viability and motility of spermatids in the previously high-dosed males.

In repeat-dose toxicity studies in dogs, slightly increased heart rate (HR), shortened RR- and PR-intervals and prolonged QT- and QTc-intervals with maximum increases of up to 60 ms were reported in individual animals, particularly at higher dose levels. Changes in heart rate and QT-interval are considered as relevant for human and are proposed to be included as a warning in the SmPC.

The variance in determining the NOAEL (28-Day tox: 10 mg/kg/day; 3-Month tox: &gt; 20 mg/kg/day) can be attributed to the extended recovery period in the 3-Month study, resulting in complete restoration of all affected organ systems except for male reproductive systems, aligning with findings from the 28-Day repeatdose study. In the 28-Day study, the intermediate dose of 20 mg/kg/day was formally recognized as the MTD. The MTD was established based on dose-limiting toxicities observed at the high dose level of 40 mg/kg/day, which was not evaluated in the 3-month study.

The design, duration, methodology, and the number of pivotal repeated-dose studies as well as the number of animals tested are sufficient to characterize the toxicological profile of resminostat. The selection of three main animal species (one non-rodent) is justified. Toxicokinetics (TK) of resminostat was evaluated in all pivotal repeated dose toxicity studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.5.4.3. Genotoxicity

The genotoxic potential of resminostat was evaluated in vitro analyzing gene mutations in bacteria (Ames test) and chromosome aberrations in Chinese hamster V79 cells and in vivo using the micronucleus test in mice after oral administration.

Positive findings in either in vitro or in vivo tests: The Ames assay demonstrated a slight mutagenic potential of resminostat, particularly in strain TA100, indicating the ability to induce gene mutations. Additionally, the in vitro study using Chinese hamster V79 D3 cells showed that resminostat induced chromosomal aberrations, supporting its clastogenic potential. In the in vivo micronucleus tests in mice, the first study indicated a potential chromosome-breaking effect of resminostat, especially in female animals at certain doses, while the second study using flow cytometry suggested non-mutagenicity. The findings from the Ames assay suggest that resminostat induces gene mutations by base pair changes, while the in vitro study on V79 D3 cells indicates its ability to cause chromosomal aberrations. These mechanisms contribute to its genotoxic potential observed across different test systems. Although a specific threshold was not identified, the Ames assay results suggest that mutagenicity occurs at higher doses of resminostat. However, the doses of 40 and 125 mg/kg resminostat have not been applied during the first and were negative in the second mouse micronucleus test.

Resminostat is considered potentially genotoxic based on the positive findings in the Ames assay and the induction of chromosomal aberrations in V79 cells. Considering the plasma exposures in mice and humans, the risk for genotoxic effects in humans is deemed low, particularly at lower doses

## 2.5.4.4. Carcinogenicity

No carcinogenicity studies were conducted with resminostat as this is not required for pharmaceuticals intended to treat patients with advanced cancer (ICH S9 Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceuticals, 2010). The Scientific Advise of 25 January 2018 concluded that \"CHMP does not agree that the proposed target population of 'patients with stage IIB-IVB MF/SS' as a whole falls under the scope of ICH S9, given the variable overall survival of the patient population, and that treatment options providing benefit are available. To enable assessment on the applicability of ICH S9 specifically for the second- and later-line population the Applicant needs to provide specific information for this setting.\"

The Applicant provided the more detailed information on the disease in different populations and disease stages. It can agree that the proposed indication 'advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS)' falls under the scope of ICH S9 and no carcinogenicity studies are required.

## 2.5.4.5. Reproductive and developmental toxicity

No fertility, early embryonic, or pre- and postnatal studies have been conducted with resminostat, as this is not required for pharmaceuticals intended for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer [ICH S9]. In addition, no studies have been conducted on embryo-fetal development because detrimental effects on embryo-fetal development have been described as a class effect of HDAC inhibitors. Therefore, it is assumed that resminostat is teratogenic. Furthermore, male and female germ cells have been identified as sites of toxicity of resminostat in repeat-dose toxicity studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.5.4.6. Toxicokinetic data

The provided TK data includes information on the doses administered to mice, rats, and dogs and the resulting plasma concentrations measured in terms of AUC and Cmax (peak plasma concentration). These parameters are essential for evaluating the pharmacokinetics and safety profile of resminostat. Based on the provided data, it appears that resminostat has been extensively studied in various animal models, with doses ranging from 5 mg/kg to 471 mg/kg in rats, 10 mg/kg to 140 mg/kg in mice, and 5 mg/kg to 80 mg/kg in dogs. The corresponding plasma concentrations (AUC and Cmax) were also measured.

The safety margin based on Fu,p was calculated in mice [28193] and dogs [28194] over a duration of 3 months, as required by the duration of clinical use to support marketing according to the EMA/CPMP/ICH/286/1995 guidelines.

Based on the in vitro fraction unbound in plasma for mouse, dog and human, the obtained safety factors of 1.45 - 5.66 indicate that the unbound resminostat levels of the rodent and non-rodent species selected for toxicity assessments are above plasma exposure levels observed in human and thus considered appropriate for evaluation of adverse effects. Additionally, when comparing the in vivo unbound plasma fraction in humans to exposures derived from in vitro data in mice and dogs, safety factors indicate comparable exposures across species for Cmax, except for a slightly higher AUC0-24 in human males than mice.

## 2.5.4.7. Local Tolerance

Dedicated local tolerance studies have not been conducted using resminostat. Resminostat was developed as a solid formulation intended for oral administrations (film-coated tablet formulation) and does not have the potential to come into contact with different sites of the human body following normal clinical use or after unintentional administration. Local tolerance was assessed as part of the repeat-dose toxicity studies.

## 2.5.4.8. Other toxicity studies

According to ICH guidelines, general toxicology studies suffice to evaluate immunotoxic potential for most anticancer pharmaceuticals, with this aspect incorporated into repeated dose toxicity studies. Separate evaluation for metabolites identified in humans is generally unnecessary for advanced cancer patients. Impurity BYK404186 (RESI-07) is justified as a metabolite of resminostat. In vitro phototoxicity study of resminostat shows concentration-dependent cytotoxicity but falls within acceptable ranges according to OECD guidelines. UV spectrum analysis indicates no absorption of light within the natural sunlight range, suggesting no phototoxic potential. Therefore, resminostat does not require further photosafety evaluations.

## 2.5.5. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

Based on the data provided, the logarithm of the partition coefficient (logKow) of resminostat mesilate fell within the acceptable range for each pH value, demonstrating favorable characteristics of the substance. Importantly, the logKow values remained significantly below the threshold level of logKow &gt; 4.5 for all tested pH values. This indicates that resminostat mesilate is unlikely to persist, bioaccumulate, or exert toxicity in the environment, as per the EU technical guidance document (TGD) referencing OSPAR.

Furthermore, the calculation of PECsurface water (PECsw) using the refined Fpen value yielded a PECsw below the threshold of concern (0.01 µg/L). Given that no other environmental concerns were identified, it is concluded that the medicinal product is unlikely to pose a significant risk to the environment when used as directed. Therefore, based on these findings, the environmental impact of the medicinal product is deemed negligible.
