## 2.5.6. Discussion on non-clinical aspects

The preclinical development of resminostat mesilate was conducted according to guidelines ICH S9 for nonclinical evaluation for anticancer pharmaceuticals and ICH M3.R2 on nonclinical safety studies for the conduct of human clinical trials and marketing authorization for pharmaceuticals.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the PD results indicate that resminostat is a potent inhibitor of class I, IIb and IV HDAC enzymes (HDAC1-11) with pronounced activity against HDAC6 at nanomolar concentrations (IC50 10 nM). In a dosedependent manner, resminostat induces acetylation of histone and non-histone proteins, which results in the transcriptional activation and/or repression of genes that mediate pleiotropic effects such as pathway modulation, cell growth arrest, differentiation, and apoptosis, including CTCL.

The mechanism of action of resminostat was established in several biochemical and cellular assays.

Specifically, in CTCL cell lines (three cell lines were used in the in vitro studies: My-La CD4+ (Mycosis fungoides, stage II), HH (Mycosis fungoides, stage IVa) and HuT78 (Sézary syndrome)), resminostat treatment: increased total protein and histone acetylation; showed anti-proliferative and pro-apoptotic activity comparable to the reference HDAC inhibitor vorinostat, with EC50 values between 0.7 and 1.2 µM; the sensitivity of resminostat against the 3 CTCL cell lines was different with HH cells demonstrating the highest sensitivity; resulted in a dose-dependent upregulation of genes associated with the Th1 phenotype (protective) and reduced expression of genes associated with the Th2 phenotype; sustainably decrease IL 31 mRNA and protein levels in My La CD4+ cells (MF) suggesting a potential for resminostat to reduce itching (pruritus) in CTCL patients.

Antitumor effects were further increased in combination with the opsonizing antibodies mogamulizumab (anti-CCR4) approved for the treatment of CTCL (FDA) and IPH4102 (anti-KIR3DL2) in development for CTCL. Overall, the increased anti-tumoral immune responses observed upon treatment with resminostat may also contribute to the clearance of CTCL cells in patients.

Analyses of resminostat's anti-proliferative activity in a large panel of human cancer cell lines did not reveal selectivity for certain cancer cells yet demonstrated differential sensitivity of the cell lines to resminostat treatment. The observed effects of resminostat were dose-dependent and long-lasting.

Overall, the presented studies are of sufficient quality to draw the conclusions of the mechanism of action of resminostat. In most cases, the statistical significance of the obtained effect was convincingly demonstrated. None of the studies had GLP status, however, it is generally acceptable that proof-of principle in-house studies are performed in a non-GLP setting, nor the GLP status is required by relevant guidelines.

In vivo, resminostat showed a broad range of antitumor activity in various orthotopic and subcutaneous xenograft mouse tumor models. However, data on the resminostat's activity in vivo are limited to lung, colon, prostate, breast, and liver tumours.

Safety pharmacology studies compliant with the ICH S7A and S7B guidelines were performed in both in vitro and in vivo test systems to assess the potential cardiovascular, respiratory, and neurobehavioral effects of resminostat after single and multiple p/o and/or i/v dose administration. The only exceptions were in vitro hERG assay and hemodynamic effect evaluation in guinea pigs. Safety pharmacology in vivo studies of resminostat were performed in mouse, rat, guinea pig, and dog. Taken together, the results did not identify a significant impact on respiratory system function (in male rats at doses up to 800 mg/kg, NOEL) or central nervous system functions (in female mice and rats NOEL was set to doses (tested both, i/v and p/o) up to 30 mg/kg, while doses of 300 mg/kg resulted in decreased activity and segregation of animals of both species for up to 1 h). Noteworthy, the behavioural effects were evaluated exclusively in female rodents while the respiratory function - in males only leaving no possibility to compare these effects between both sexes of animals.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There were no safety pharmacology findings of concern following single doses, except the transient prolongation of QT, partially accompanied by decreased heart rates, but without correlation to plasma levels of resminostat. The hemodynamic effects of cumulative infusions of resminostat were analyzed in anaesthetized guinea pigs (non-GLP study). The effects on cardiovascular parameters after single and multiple oral administration of resminostat in conscious telemetered dogs (both sexes). In both species, slight decreases of blood pressure were reported more frequently at higher doses. The slight to moderate increases in heart rate (early tachycardia) were noted after treatment with resminostat - this effect was not related to the resminostat dose, it was reversible and considered of minor clinical relevance.

Transient shortening of QT and QTc intervals was observed early (within 8.5 h) after treatment and transient prolongation of QT was observed late (within 13-22 h) after dosing in dogs, partially accompanied by decreased heart rates, but without correlation to plasma levels of resminostat. However, in clinical studies, there were a few clinically significant ECG findings, such as permanent atrial fibrillation or signs of a previous myocardial infarction, though no correlation between ∆QTcF and resminostat plasma concentrations was observed. The calculated IC50 in the hERG inhibition assay is 245 μM, which indicates a potential for QT prolongation. The validation of the non-GLP hERG assay results is questionable. However, considering that dedicated safety pharmacology studies are not warranted as per ICH S9 for this indication, this issue was not pursued. Moreover, the in vitro observed QT prolongation is consistent with the observed in vivo effects. It is known that HDAC inhibitors as a class effect induce QT interval prolongation, and this was also demonstrated for resminostat.

In conclusion, none of the risks evaluated in the safety pharmacology program were identified as clinically relevant (considering the doses chosen for administration in humans), which can be agreed.

No in-depth screening for off-target effects of resminostat was performed to provide information on secondary effects via binding to a selection of extracellular receptors or ion channels. Since the obtained results from toxicology studies and clinical trials have shown that there is no indication for secondary interactions with main receptor systems, the performed study portfolio is deemed satisfactory and the choice of omitting further in-depth non-clinical secondary PD studies is appropriate.

The applicant evaluated and reported on the additive to synergistic effects of resminostat mesilate in combination with irinotecan and 5-FU in three CRC cell lines. Gene expression data provided mechanistic insights and suggested that resminostat has the potential for re-sensitization of resistant cells to treatment with 5-FU. In vivo combination with irinotecan was further confirmed also in a colon cancer xenograft mouse model showing the potential to almost completely inhibition of tumor growth. A similar effect was demonstrated by combining resminostat mesilate and kinase inhibitor sorafenib in orthotopic xenograft mouse model of human liver cancer. Thus, there is sufficient evidence provided to acknowledge the synergistic anticancer effect of the drug combination. The Applicant has not further discussed how the obtained data could be extrapolated to the CTCL treatment options in humans.

Since no appropriate animal models have been used for resminostat anti-CTLC efficacy testing, it is agreed that the antitumor activity of resminostat in the proposed indication CTCL was based on the obtained in vitro analyses only and the presented in vivo data can only be used for consideration. Noteworthy, the human cancer cell lines chosen for xenografting were among those that were used for the evaluation of MoA of resminostat in vitro and the obtained in vivo study results generally were in agreement with the in vitro data - tumour development was suppressed in a dose-dependent manner in experimental animals, although mechanistically the responsible MoA for resminostat was not further elaborated in these in vivo studies. For example, in vitro data suggested that treatment with resminostat strongly increased the expression of CD56 and CD69 markers reflecting NK cell activation and demonstrated increased cytotoxicity against cancer cells as well as the possible protective role of ADCC mechanism when resminostat was used in combination with opsonizing antibodies; however, no NK-mediated effects of resminostat were assessed in the selected in vivo models to validate the in vitro effects, which is a shortcoming. Moreover, the chosen rodent models (immunodeficient mice lacking either functional T/B cells or T/B/NK cells) inherently cannot validate the obtained in vitro data regarding the immune system-mediated MoA of resminostat (Th1/Th2 balance, T-cell mediated effects).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Another issue identified was the use of exclusively female mice, omitting males in the in vivo studies. Considering the known differences in CTCL prevalence between males and females (more frequent in males), it would have been of even higher importance to assess sex as an independent variable in the in vivo studies evaluating resminostat anticancer efficacy. Another fact that adds to this issue is the identified differences in systemic exposure of the resminostat (data from the performed safety pharmacology and PK studies). Despite the fact that non-CTCL animal models were used for resminostat testing, addressing sex as an independent variable would have been of value for translating the in vivo results to clinics.

In addition, the presented in vivo studies demonstrated limited repeatability of the results and/or lack of statistical power.

Finally, the in vitro hERG assay resulted in QT prolongation, which is consistent with the observed in vivo effects. It is known that HDAC inhibitors, as a class effect, induce QT interval prolongation, and this was also demonstrated for resminostat.

A comprehensive PK program was conducted to evaluate the pharmacokinetics, absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion properties for resminostat in mouse, rat, dog and monkey. Species and strains used for pharmacokinetic evaluation were the same as for pharmacology and toxicology evaluations and identical resminostat formulations were administered.

Analytical methods. Plasma concentrations of resminostat free base were determined by HPLC-MS/MS methods that were developed and validated for mouse, rat, dog and human plasma. For monkey plasma, a qualified (unvalidated) HPLC-MS/MS method was applied, which was shown to be reliable to produce credible results. Adequate calibration ranges and synchronous internal standards were chosen for all test species allowing proper comparison of the obtained results. For toxicokinetic analyses, plasma samples of the pivotal (GLP-compliant) repeat dose toxicity studies of oral resminostat mesilate were analyzed using the validated methods only. The radiochemical purity of radiolabeled [14C]-resminostat mesilate was sufficient; methods of appropriate sensitivity were chosen for the detection of radiolabeled resminostat in biological samples.

Absorption. The presented in vitro data with human colorectal adenocarcinoma cell (Caco-2 cell) monolayers suggests intermediate moderate-to-high permeability of resminostat without major permeability limitation in intestinal absorption. The efflux ratio was defined as 1.45 indicating no active transport of resminostat in the Caco-2 cells. Appropriate study design was applied with all the controls necessary for the assessment.

In vivo resminostat as single dose was administered either orally (mouse, rat, dog, monkey) or intravenously (dog, monkey) only in male animals, which is a shortcoming. Resminostat after oral administration was rapidly and almost completely absorbed in mouse (92%) and dog (82%) and well absorbed in rat (up to 72%, only if high dose of 150 mg/kg was used) with time of occurrence of maximum plasma concentration (tmax) values in the range of 0.6 - 2.0 h. Oral bioavailability was determined in dog (67%) and monkey (89%).

Data from pivotal GLP-compliant 3-month oral repeat-dose toxicity studies showed that systemic exposure to resminostat is dose-related and linear in both males and females in mouse and dog. However, in mouse systemic exposure was higher in female than in male animals at all dose levels. The mean plasma elimination half-life of resminostat ranged from 0.83 - 3.47 h. No accumulation of resminostat was reported. In dog, the mean plasma elimination half-life of resminostat ranged from 0.98 - 1.21 h. Accumulation factors of 1.06 - 1.24 for males and 1.28 - 1.79 for females were reported across all dose groups. No gender differences were noted in rats.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Distribution. After administration of radiolabeled resminostat to mouse and rat, radioactivity was rapidly and widely distributed to various tissues with a similar pattern of distribution. In order of declining exposure, large intestine, followed by liver, kidneys and adrenals were most exposed to radioactivity after oral administration while large intestine, followed by liver, kidneys and spleen were most exposed after intravenous administration of [14C]-resminostat.

In partially pigmented Lister Hooded rat after oral administration, [14C]-resminostat showed highest exposures in the uveal tract/retina, liver, stomach mucosa, bile ducts, skin (pigmented) and preputial gland. Exposure to drug-related radioactivity in the melanin-rich uveal tract/retina of partially pigmented rats was higher than in any other tissue, suggesting that melanin binding is prevalent. Low levels of radioactivity were detected in the brain.

No distinct retention of drug-related material was observed in any organ or tissue as assessed for up to 48 h (mouse) and 168 h (rat) post-dose.

Distribution of resminostat was assessed only in males, which is a shortcoming.

A mean plasma protein binding of 85.0% was determined for resminostat mesilate in human after single oral doses during a clinical mass balance study, with a mean fraction unbound of 15.0%. In vitro, plasma protein binding was observed in mouse, rat, dog, and human plasma with around 30% of unbound fraction in mouse, rat, and dog and 6.7% in human. The mean human blood/plasma-partitioning coefficient was 1.03 (10 min incubation) and 0.954 (60 min incubation).

The absence of placental and milk transfer data of resminostat is regarded dispensable given the envisaged patient population.

Metabolism. In vitro data from liver microsomes and hepatocytes model suggested a low proclivity of resminostat to hepatic biotransformation in the non-clinical species and human - resminostat showed low to moderate clearance. However, in vivo physiologically scaled plasma clearances was shown to be higher than in vitro in dog (2.2 L/h × kg versus 0.71 L/h × kg in vitro), monkey (3.5 L/h × kg versus 0.55 L/h × kg in vitro), and human (0.72 L/h × kg versus 0.29 L/h × kg in vitro). Resminostat was rapidly and extensively metabolized in vivo, thus it can be concluded that the used standard in vitro models of metabolic stability were not predictive for the in vivo behavior of resminostat.

Incubations with recombinant human CYP enzymes revealed CYP2C8 as the major CYP enzyme for metabolic degradation of resminostat and several metabolites formed by almost all CYP enzymes tested. Resminostat is metabolized predominantly at the hydroxamic acid group, leading to formation of various metabolites. In addition to hydroxamic acid modifications, direct glucuronidation of the parent was identified to be a metabolic pathway of resminostat in the tested species.

Overall, the in vitro and in vivo results from studies in mouse, rat, dog, monkey and human suggest several metabolic pathways for resminostat. The major metabolites observed at ≥ 5% in non-clinical in vivo studies in plasma, urine and/or feces included M4-gluc, M7, M11, M12, M15 and M16 and the taurine conjugates M30 and M33. In human, the major metabolites identified at ≥ 5% were M14-gluc, M15, M4-gluc, M16 and M39. All of these metabolites as well as their proposed pathway-related intermediate products were also detected in experimental animals indicating similar or identical metabolic pathways, except for M14-gluc, the major plasma metabolite in human.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Of note, the metabolites M7, M10, M12 and M16 were tested in vitro for their inhibitory activity against HDACs and none of these metabolites was shown to be pharmacologically active, besides, no accumulation was observed for any of the metabolites. Thus, it can be agreed that none of the metabolic pathways could result in significant deviations from the expected pharmacological effects of resminostat.

Excretion. Appropriate methodological approaches were chosen for the evaluation of resminostat excretion in vivo - administration of radiolabeled resminostat mesilate was performed orally and intravenously to mouse, rat and dog. Excretion of resminostat was demonstrated via both feces and urine, with around 60% and 30% in mouse, 50% and 50% in rat, and 25% and 70% in dog determined for fecal and urinary excretion, respectively, after oral administration thus demonstrating species difference. For all routes and doses a complete or near-complete recovery of the radioactivity administered was observed within the sampling periods of 48 h (mouse), 72 h (rat), and 168 h (dog).

Pharmacokinetic drug interactions. In vitro data revealed that resminostat is a substrate of CYP2C8 (expected to be responsible for up to 86% of hepatic metabolism), but also of CYP1A2 and 2C19 indicating alternative metabolic pathways for resminostat (confirmed via human mass balance study). Based on the obtained results, CYPs are considered the major enzymes contributing to ≥ 25% of overall elimination of resminostat. No additional phase I metabolizing enzymes have been tested, which is in line with current EMA guidlines. However, in vivo DDIs related to inhibition or induction of one of the metabolic pathways such as CYP2C8 activity (resminostat as victim) are expected to be unlikely due to the observed variety of metabolic pathways and the wide therapeutic range of resminostat reported during clinical trials.

According to in vitro data, resminostat is a substrate of the P-gp.

In vitro transporter interaction data revealed that resminostat inhibited the hepatic uptake transporters OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OCT1. No interaction was reported for the efflux transporter BCRP. A small inhibition was noted towards the renal uptake transporters OAT1, OAT3 and OCT2. The renal transporters multidrug and toxin extrusion protein (MATE) 1 and MATE2-K have not been tested, which is agreed since these tests are not mandatory according to current EMA guidelines.

Static modeling of the obtained in vitro data did not indicate a risk of in vivo DDIs related to inhibition of P-gp (MDR1), OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OCT1. Based on the current EMA thresholds, the risk for in vivo DDIs related to inhibition of the hepatic uptake transporters OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OCT1 by resminostat cannot be fully excluded. However, inhibition kinetics indicate &gt; 85% (OATP1B1, OATP1B3) and &gt; 90% (OCT1) of transporter activity remaining at the expected hepatic concentrations of resminostat. Based on all other guidances (FDA, PMDA and ICH M12 draft), in vivo DDIs related to inhibition of hepatic uptake transporters are unlikely, which is agreed.

Taken together, the DDI potential of resminostat was assessed based on in vitro data of transporter and metabolizing enzyme interactions and clinical data on exposure, metabolism and safety and subject to static modeling. Dynamic data such as enzyme kinetics and rapid metabolic degradation of resminostat were taken into account to estimate the risk of in vivo DDIs. In conclusion, it is agreed that in vivo DDIs related to the tested transporter and enzyme interactions of resminostat are considered unlikely.

Justification of omission of the NC placental transfer studies is based on test article teratogenic properties and significant effects on human reproduction. Argumentation of the Applicant and related awareness measures in PI are acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The most important findings from the non-clinical studies on resminostat include dose-dependent effects observed in non-GLP single-dose studies. Single dose toxicity studies were conducted in rats and dogs administered resminostat orally by gavage or as a capsule. No mortality or significant clinical signs were observed in any animal species or dose group. Resminostat was well tolerated in both species. The target organs of toxicity were similar in rats and dogs: hemolymphopoietic system and gastrointestinal tract. Resminostat administered orally by gavage and capsules to beagle dogs caused marked vomiting. Capsule administration did not reduce the emetic effect, but the systemic exposure was lower than the plasma AUCs obtained after administration of the same dose by gavage. The dose of 200 mg/kg (157.0 mg), corresponding to an AUC of 17130 to 20966 μg·h/L, can be considered as the maximum tolerated dose (MTD). Vomiting was considered the dose-limiting toxicity in this study. Mice, rats and dogs were studied in non-pivotal repeat-dose studies, but mice and dogs were selected as the rodent and non-rodent species for pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies and toxicokinetic evaluation. In nonpivotal studies, rats were found to be less exposed to resminostat following oral administration. Mouse and dog have comparable steady state AUCs based on dose per body surface area, appropriate pharmacokinetic profiles and metabolism. The chronic toxicity of resminostat has been assessed in repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in mice and dogs for up to 13 weeks. Resminostat was administered by oral gavage once daily in mice and dogs. Repeat-dose toxicity studies showed a broadly consistent profile of toxicities that were dose- dependent. Target organs for resminostat toxicity included the hemolymphopoietic system, the gastrointestinal tract and the reproductive organs. No treatment-related deaths were observed. The studies showed dose-related decreases in hematological parameters at all doses. Decreases in lymphocytes were seen at the 70 mg/kg dose in the 13-week study, but at all doses in the 28-day study. All effects were generally nearly reversible during the recovery period. At the 140 mg/kg dose, a slight decrease in red blood cells parameters (erythrocyte count, hematocrit, haemoglobin) and thrombocytes was also observed that was recovered by day 43. Histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic system at all doses included lymphoid depletion of the thymus and lymph nodes, increased severity of extramedullary hemopoiesis of the spleen. Reproductive toxicities in male animals included decreased testicular weights, germ cell depletion and decreased spermatozoa in the testes and epididymides at a dose of 140 mg/kg/day. These changes were not completely reversible after the recovery period (germ cell depletion, spermatozoa reduction, intratubular degenerated germ cells were reported). In female mice, changes in the ovaries (follicular atrophy), were observed in all dose groups, and atrophy of the uterus and vagina, reduced (relative and absolute) weight of the ovaries were observed at 140 mg/kg/day. After the recovery period, only animals in the 140 mg/kg/day group still showed findings in the ovaries; in all other groups these changes were completely reversible within 14 days. In the 28-day study the dose of 140 mg/kg/day was considered to be the MTD (maximum tolerated dose). Based on the low incidence and almost complete reversibility of hemolymphopoietic system and genital tract changes, the NOAEL was determined to be 35 mg/kg/day, with an exposure multiple of 0.26-fold the human AUC. In the 3-month study decreases in leukocytes, platelets and neutrophils were observed at all dose levels, but a decrease in lymphocytes was observed at the high dose of 70 mg/kg/day. In the 3-month study in mice, there were no findings in the reproductive organs of male and female animals and no histopathological changes in the hemolymphopoietic system are detected. However, the effects of repeated doses of

resminostat mesilate at 35 and 70 mg/kg/day were comparable between the 28-day and 3-month studies with regards to the targets of toxicity and severity. In dogs, resminostat was tested at doses of 10, 20 and 40 mg/kg/day for 28 days (+ 2-week recovery) and at doses of 5, 10 and 20 mg/kg/day for 13 weeks (+ 4week recovery). Treatment-associated deaths were observed in 1 female dog at dose 40 mg/kg due to toxicity in the hemolymphopoietic system.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the 28-day study increased toxicity was observed at the high dose of 40 mg/kg/day. The doses of 10 mg and 20 mg kg/day were well tolerated. Reduced leukocytes, red blood cells, reticulocytes and thrombocytes were observed at all dose levels. These changes were reversible during the recovery period. Lymphoid depletion was also observed in the thymus at all doses, in the lymph nodes at 20 -40 mg/kg/day and in the spleen at 40 mg/kg/day. Lymphoid depletion in thymus was noted even after the recovery period.

In all dose groups, germ cell depletion in the testes, oligospermia in the epididymides and atrophy of the prostate were observed in males and changes in the ovaries, uterus, and mammary glands in females. Changes in the male genital tract were observed even after the recovery period. The NOAEL was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day for treatment period of 4 weeks. On Day 28 Cmax and AUC0-24 values were 1.145 µg/mL and 1.718 µg·hr/mL, respectively with an exposure multiple of 4.57-fold human AUC. The dose of 20 mg/kg/day was considered to be the formal MTD.

Additionally, findings from genotoxicity studies indicated a slight mutagenic potential in the Ames assay and chromosomal aberrations in in vitro studies. However, the risk of genotoxic effects at the dose levels administered to humans is considered to be low. The Applicant did not perform the carcinogenicity studies in line with ICH S9. In line with the Scientific Advise of 25 January 2018 the Applicant has provided more detailed information on the disease in different populations and disease stages. It can be agreed that the proposed indication 'advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS)' falls under the scope of ICH S9 and no carcinogenicity studies are required.

No specific fertility and early embryonic development toxicity studies were conducted. Reproductive organs of mice, rats, and dogs were assessed in the repeat dose toxicity studies. Resminostat should not be used during pregnancy, which is proposed to be reflected in section 4.6 of the SPC.

No embryofetal development studies were conducted. Considering the teratogenicity as the class effect of HDAC inhibitors, this can be accepted.

No pre- and postnatal development studies were conducted. In line with ICH S9 and the intended indications, this can be accepted.

Resminostat did not demonstrate phototoxicity risk in the in vitro Neutral Red uptake assay. While the nonclinical study program for resminostat included a comprehensive set of studies, including single-dose, repeatdose toxicity, and genotoxicity studies, the risk-based approach in study selection is not explicitly stated.

The Predicted Environmental Concentration in surface water (PECsurfacewater) was calculated using a refined Fpen, which is considered acceptable given the prevalence of CTCL in Europe. Based on these assumptions, the PECsurfacewater is calculated to be 0.00807 µg/L, which is below the action limit of 0.01 µg/L. Resminostat is not classified as an endocrine active substance. A Phase II assessment is not warranted. The log of the n-octanol/water partition coefficient (log Pow) of resminostat was determined to be -0.85 at pH 5, 0.74 at pH7 and -0.22 at pH 9. These values are considered to be well below the threshold level of log Kow &gt; 4.5. No further PBT assessment is required.

The active substance is a natural substance, the use of which will not alter the concentration or distribution of the substance in the environment. Therefore resminostat is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Assessment of paediatric data on non-clinical aspects

Not applicable

## 2.5.7. Conclusion on the non-clinical aspects

Overall, the PD results indicate that resminostat is a potent inhibitor of class I, IIb and IV HDAC enzymes with pronounced activity against HDAC6 at nanomolar concentrations. There were no safety pharmacology findings of concern following single doses, except the transient prolongation of QT, partially accompanied by decreased heart rates, but without correlation to plasma levels of resminostat. The PK of resminostat has been studied in a large panel of studies elucidating ADME and DDI features of orally applied resminostat mesilate. From the toxicological point of view, non-clinical studies highlight resminostat's dose-dependent effects and potential genotoxicity. The toxicological profile of resminostat has been adequately addressed by the studies submitted.

## 2.6.1. Introduction

## GCP aspects

The Clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant

The applicant has provided a statement to the effect that clinical trials conducted outside the Community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

## 2.6.2. Clinical pharmacology

## 2.6.2.1. Pharmacokinetics

Resminostat (resminostat mesilate) is an oral selective inhibitor of class I, IIb and IV histone deacetylases (HDAC) enzymes. The intended commercial pharmaceutical formulation for this drug product is an immediate-release film-coated tablets with one dose strength of 200 mg.

The proposed recommended oral dose is 600 mg (3 tablets of 200 mg resminostat) administered once daily (QD) in repeated 14-day treatment cycles, each consisting of 5 days of once daily resminostat administration followed by a 9-day resminostat-free period (the \"5 + 9\" schedule).

Resminostat has a molecular weight of 445.51 g/mol. The molecular formula is C17H23N3O7S2. The compound is achiral.

In support to the clinical pharmacology program, the Applicant submitted, additionally to in vitro investigations (in vitro metabolite profiling, CYP inhibition and induction, Caco-2 permeability evaluation and protein binding …), PK and PD data from 8 clinical studies, including one pivotal study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) in target patients with advanced Cutaneous T-Cell Lymphomas (CTCL).

Two population PK (Pop-PK) analyses were performed: an initial Pop-PK analysis (report 4SC-201-PoPPK003) conducted to determine the optimal sparse sampling strategy for the phase 2 pivotal study RESMAIN in advanced MF and SS, and a final popPK analysis using a revised dataset from six clinical studies, including the pivotal Resmain study.

No specific exposure-response analysis was conducted at the time of the present submission. However, a PK/PD modeling analysis based on results from the FIM Study 7341/EM-001 (report 4SC-201-PKPD-004) and a concentration-ECG effect analyses for resminostat was performed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Methods

Different bioanalytical methods (HPLC-MS/MS) were developed to quantify resminostat free base in human plasma with K-EDTA as anticoagulant.

## Absorption

After single or multiple dose administration of resminostat in both healthy subjects (food Study YHI-1001-FE07) and CTCL patients (pivotal Study 4SC-201-6-2015) using the intended commercial tablets, the median Tmax was achieved at 1h (range: 0.5 to 2 h); indicating that absorption of resminostat is rapid. This is supported by similar Tmax values observed in patients with oncologic indications and using others formulations.

Based on the Pop-PK model, at C1D5, after a multiple dose of 600 mg QD (given as 3 tablets each containing 200 mg) in patients with CTCL, the geometric mean Cmax and AUC0-24h are expected to be 5012 ng/mL and 16187 ng.h/mL, respectively.

## Absolute Bioavailability

The absolute bioavailability of resminostat has not been investigated.

Based on the results from the mass balance study 4SC-201-7-2020, a readable absorption (&gt;85%) of resminostat can be concluded as approximately 85.4% (±2.6%) of the administered dose was recovered in urine. In addition, most of the 11.8% (± 2.3%) of the dose recovered in feces occurred after 24 to 36h, suggesting that this amount was absorbed and then excreted by biliary route rather than due to unabsorbed drug.

## Relative Bioavailability

Different resminostat formulations and strengths were used during the pharmaceutical development. The formulation proposed for commercial use is a film coated tablet (one strength 200 mg of resminostat free base) that have been used in the target population for B/R demonstration and also in the phase 1 food effect study. This formulation showed an increased bioavailability of approximately 20 to 50% for Cmax and AUCs compared to the initial formulations.

## Influence of food

The effect of a high fat meal on the PK of resminostat was investigated in a dedicated formal Study YHI1001-FE-07. The study included 12 healthy volunteers who received, in a crossover design, a single oral dose of 200 mg resminostat as a film-coated tablet (the intended commercial formulation) under fasting and fed states. PK results indicated that administration of a high fat meal increased slightly the geometric means of AUClast and Cmax by 9.9% and 6.3%, respectively. The median Tmax was not affected by food and remains unchanged at around 1h under both conditions. Based on these data, resminostat can be administered regardless of food intake.

## Influence of gastric modifier food

The effect of pH modulators such as antiacids, PPI or H2 receptor antagonists on the systemic exposure of resminostat was not discussed.

## Distribution

Based on in vitro investigations (Report ADM-22-4066b), Resminostat was found to be moderately bound (93.3 ±0.6) to human plasma proteins. In vivo results confirm that resminostat is moderately bound to plasma proteins with an estimated mean fraction of unbound resminostat in plasma of 15.0% (protein binding of about 85%). Resminostat binds preferentially to human serum albumin (HSA). At 10 μM, the bound fraction was approximately 76.3% in HSA and 11.6% in alpha-1-acid glycoprotein (AGP).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The geometric mean blood-to-plasma ratio for the total radioactivity was 0.26 less than 1, indicating limit penetration of resminostat or its metabolites into red blood cells.

Based on the mass balance Study, the geometric mean Vz/F (SD) of resminostat was estimated at 162 (22.2) in healthy volunteers. In the Pop-PK analysis (2-compartement model), the mean apparent volume of distribution (Vc/F) was found to be significantly affected by age. The geometric mean apparent Vz/F was estimated at 121.6 L in target patients with advanced MF and SS. Overall, both non-compartmental analysis and Pop-PK results suggest a high tissue distribution of resminostat.

## Elimination

## Metabolism

In vitro data (incubation in liver microsomes and recombinant human CYP enzymes) indicated that resminostat is mainly metabolized by CYP2C8 (86.0%) and minor contributions of CYP1A2 (10.8%) and CYP2C19 (3.1%).

Resminostat underwent extensive metabolism that appears to be primarily displayed by hydrolysis, hydrogenation, glucuronidation, oxidative cleavage, demethylation and methylation, all of which contributed to the formation of several resminostat metabolites.

Following a single oral dose of [14C] resminostat, unchanged resminostat (parent drug) accounted for only approximately 10.5% of the total drug-related systemic exposure (0 to 48 hours) and a total of 35 potential metabolites were detected in plasma. The most abundant circulating component was the M14/P21 glucuronide metabolite (oxidative cleavage and glucuronidation of resminostat) representing 15.1% of the total exposure. Then, the metabolites M16, M4 (resminostat glucuronide), M15 (oxidative cleavage and reduction of resminostat) and M39 (desmethyl M16) accounting for around 7%, 7%, 8% and 7% of the total exposure, respectively. All other metabolites detected in plasma accounted for 1% to 5% of the total exposure.

In urine, the unchanged drug accounted for 6% of the total administered dose (0 to 48 hours) and up to 43 possible metabolites were detected. The most abundant metabolites were characterized as M16 (20%), M39 (13%), and M15 (10%). In feces, a total of 32 metabolites were detected and M15 was the most abundant metabolite accounting for around 4% of the dose.

## Excretion

Following oral administration, resminostat is predominantly excreted by the kidneys with complete recovery (mean ±SD of 97.2% ± 0.4%) of the dose achieved in urine (85.4% ± 2.59%) and faeces (11.8% ± 2.29%).

The renal clearance of resminostat was not calculated or could not be found.

Very rapid t1/2 elimination in healthy volunteers (1.77 h and 2.2 h in the food effect and ADME studies, respectively) and in patients with advanced solid malignancy or malignant lymphomas (mean values ranging from 1.5 h to 3.8 h) was initially found.

Based on the Pop-PK analysis (two-compartment model), the elimination of resminostat is shown to be biphasic with an initial mean (CI 95%) t1/2 of 1.9 h (1.8-2.0 h) followed by terminal mean (95% CI) t1/2 of approximately 29 hours (range 24-34) in the target patients with advanced-stage of MF or SS. Following multiple dose of resminostat 600 mg QD in patients, the geometric mean apparent oral clearance (CL/F) is 47.6 L/h.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Dose proportionality and time dependencies

Following ascending single or multiple doses in patients with advanced malignancies, the systemic exposures of resminostat appear to exhibit a more than dose proportional increase across the dose range [100-800 mg]. On one hand, after single dosing, the geometric mean Cmax and AUClast were 487 ng/mL and 1230 ng.h/mL at 100 mg versus 4970 ng/mL and 21100 ng.h/mL at 800 mg, respectively; indicating an increase of AUC by more than 2-fold the increase of dose. On the other hand, mean apparent CL/F clearly decreases with increasing dose levels: CL/F was 80.9 mL/h at 100 mg versus 37.7 mL/h at 800 mg.

PK data after repeated 600 mg QD administrations following the \"5 + 9\" schedule did not indicate any evident trend of accumulation of the systemic exposure of resminostat (Racc near to 1 for both AUCtau and Cmax) after repeated cycles. The lack of time dependency appears consistent with the elimination t1/2 of resminostat (around 30 hours) and particularly given the 9-day resminostat-free period within the proposed \"5 + 9\" dosing schema that allow theoretically a complete elimination of resminostat between cycles of 14 days.

Steady state achievement remains unclear as no PK data (Ctrough, Cmax, AUC24h) after more than 5 days of treatment were available. Theoretically, steady state is expected by day 6 to 7 after continuous daily treatment.

## Pharmacokinetics in target population

The PKs of resminostat in the target patients with advanced MF or SS, following the claimed 600 mg QD dosing according to the \"5 + 9\" schedule and using the to-be marketed tablets, was evaluated, as a monotherapy, in one pivotal phase 2 study 4SC-201-6-2015 (Resmain).

No non-compartmental analysis calculation was performed given the sparse PK sampling (3 samples at C3D1 0.75, 2 and 4h post dose and 3 samples at C3D5 predose, 2 and 7h post dose). Therefore, the available data (489 samples from 91 patients) were pooled in the population PK dataset and PK parameters / exposures metrics of resminostat were estimated using the Pop-PK analysis.

Resminostat concentration-time profiles after single dosing (C1D1) and repeated dosing (C1D5) of 600 mg QD are plotted in Figure 1. In patients, model-based exposures predictions are summarised in Table 8. The geometric mean Cmax was 5012.2 ng/mL, the AUC was 16187.5 ng.h/mL at day 5 post dose after repeated QD 600 mg dosing. The geometric means of CL/F and the terminal t1/2 were estimated at 47.6 mL/h and 29 h.

## Population Pharmacokinetic analysis

A revised population pharmacokinetic analysis aiming to characterize the PK of resminostat and identify / quantify sources of variability was developed and submitted as part of the Applicant's Day 120 responses. The population PK dateset was expanded by including roll over patients from the pivotal Resmain study. From this new analysis, the predicted systemic exposure metrics for the target population were obtained.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The PPK of resminostat was based on PK data pooling 6 clinical studies including healthy volunteers (YHI1001-FE-07) and patients of the target population (RESMAIN) which received the commercial formulation (Tablet formulation 4), and patients (YHI-1001-ST-01, 7341/EM-001, 4SC-201-2-2009, and 4SC-2011-2009) treated by resminostat for other indications and using initial clinical formulations (Capsules and tablet formulation 1).

Covariates of interest were baseline demographic covariates (age, body weight, gender, race/ethnicity), formulation, dose, and renal function (CrCL).

PPK was built using nonlinear mixed effects model with the SAEM algorithm for parameter estimation implemented in NONMEM (version 7.5.1, ICON Development Solutions, Ellicott City, MD). PK parameters was assumed to follow a log-normal distribution. RUV (residual unexplained variability) was explored using standard error models (additive, proportional or combined). Covariate effects were first explored graphically, then testing of the covariate effects was performed using a SCM building strategy (single addition, forward inclusion and backward elimination) with p&lt;0.01 for inclusion and p&lt;0.01 for exclusion. The PPK model was evaluated using standard diagnostic plots, visual predictive check and bootstrap.

Overall 3914 observations from 228 subjects were included. Table 9 provide the final PK parameter estimates and Figure 2 and Figure 3 the associated GOF plots and VPC. The predicted PK parameters for the target population are summarised in Table 8.

- a time varying absorption with a lag time (double weibull function) to describe the absorption of the capsule or the tablet formulation 1, or
- a zero-order process with a lag time to describe the absorption of the commercial formulation parameterized by D2 (duration of the zero-order absorption process), ALAG2 (lag time of the zero-order absorption process) and FF1 (relative bioavailaibiity of the commercial formulation with respect to the clinical formulations).

The fixed effects estimates for the final model were for CL/F, V/F, Kam, LAG2, D2 anf F2 were 20.5 L/h, 38.1 L, 1.24 h-1, 0.38 h, 0.18 h and 1.27h. IIV was considered on all the PK parameters and was moderate to low for CL/F (33%) and low for V/F (24%) and F2 (0%). For the absorption related parameters associated to the double weibull function IIV ranged from 51% (Lag) to 101% for TD1. For the parameters associate to the zero-order absorption model with lag-time, IIV was moderate to very high with 36% for LAG2 and 148% for D2. Finally for the parameters characterizing the second compartment IIV was high ranging from 112 to 158%. Overall all main fixed effects were estimated with a good precision (RSE&lt;20% except for some of the parameters from the double weibull function and for D2). A significant effect of body weight on both CL/F and V/F was identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, GOF plots suggested that the model describe adequately the PK data. The VPCs showed that the model performs well in each study as the 5th, the 50th and the 95th percentiles were reasonably well predicted indicating that the population model properly described the observed data in each of the six trials included in the analysis.

## Special populations

## Impaired renal function

No formal dedicated PK study was performed to investigate the effect of renal impairment on resminostat PKs.

In the PK population analysis, subjects were characterized with regard to renal status using the creatinine clearance metric (CLCR as expressed mL/min) calculated by the Cockroft-Gault formula. The Pop-PK dataset included n=135 (59.2%), n=75 (32.9%), n=18 (7.8%) and n=0 (0%) patients with normal renal function, mild, moderate and severe renal impairment, respectively. The effect of renal impairment as continuous covariate was evaluated in Pop-PK model and was not retained in the final model as a significant covariate on resminostat PKs. Therefore, it was concluded that no significant impact on the PK of resminostat is to be expected in patients with mild and moderate renal impaired function and no dose adjustment is proposed in these two subgroups of patients.

## Impaired hepatic function

No formal dedicated PK study was conducted to assess the effect of hepatic impairment on resminostat PK parameters and the effect of hepatic impairment was not evaluated within the Pop-PK approach.

## Gender

The effect of gender on the PKs of resminostat was investigated within the population PK analysis. Approximately 62.7% (n= 143 of 228) male vs 37.3% female (85 of 228) of patients included in the population dataset. Data issued from the target population appear well-balanced between men and women (48 and 43 patients of 91, respectively). In the covariate analysis, no significant dependency between PK parameters of resminostat and the gender was detected and then the covariate was not retained in the final model.

## Race / Ethnicity

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study YHI-1001-ST-01

A dedicated phase has been performed to evaluate the efficacy, safety and PKPD of resminostat in patients of Japanese ethnicity (n= 12) with solid tumours. 3 doses levels were tested: 400 mg, 600 and 800 mg, with resminostat administered once daily according to the \"5 + 9\" schedule. The study was supported by tablet formulation 1 (not the commercial tablets). The median tmax was achieved at around 1.3 to 1.9 h and the geometric mean of CL/F ranged between 24.9 to 51 mL/h. In the 600 mg cohort, the geometric means of Cmax and AUC24h after single dose were 6020 ng/mL and 24000 ng.h/mL, respectively

## Pop-PK modelling

The effect of ethnicity on the PKs of resminostat was assessed within the Pop-PK analysis. The analysed dataset included a majority of Caucasian (94.7%, n=216 of 228) and n= 12 (5.3%) Japanese patients. The covariate analyses suggested no effect on PK parameters of resminostat based on ethnicity. Additional VPC plots at day 5 were provided and show similar observed and simulated systemic exposure on resminostat in both populations (Caucasian versus Japanese) following the same dosage regimen.

## Body weight

No formal studies were conducted to investigate the effect of body weight (BW) on the PK of resminostat.

The body weight effect was investigated within the population PK analysis. Among the 228 participants included in the population PK dataset, mean BW was 73.2 kg (median = 73 kg, min = 35 kg, max = 113 kg). Out of the 91 patients with MF and SS, mean BW was 75.9 kg (median = 75 kg, min = 50 kg, max = 109.2 kg). BW was found to be a statistically significant covariate on both resminostat's CL/F and V/F and retained in the final Pop-PK model. PK simulations indicate clearly that the systemic exposure of resminostat (Cmax, AUC) increases as patient's weight decreases and vice versa. For patients weighting between 50 to 100 kg, the geometric mean ratios (and 95%CI) of the log-transformed exposure parameters Cmax und AUC compared to the reference BW of 74 kg were within the interval [0.8-1.25], indicating no clinically significant difference in exposures of resminostat for patients with a BW between 50-100kg. However, the mean resminostat exposure at steady state (AUCss) is expected to be increased by approximately 49.5% (26114/17459 ng∙hr/mL) and by 37% (24024/17459 ng∙hr/mL) for patients with low weigh of 30 and 40 kg respectively; and in contrast high BW of 110kg and 120 kg would result on a reduced AUCss in average by approximately 21.3% (13751/17459 ng∙hr/mL) and 27.4% (12684/17459 ng∙hr/mL), respectively after repeated daily dose of 600 mg resminostat. Overall, it was concluded that no dosing adjustment is warranted based on BW.

## Age / Elderly

No formal investigations in elderly was performed.

The effect of age on the PKs of resminostat was investigated within the population PK analysis and was not found to be a statistically significant covariate. The impact of age (40, 60, and 80 years old) on the expected resminostat PK parameters (Tmax, Cmax, Cmin, Caverage, AUC0-24h, t1/2) at day 5 using the commercial tablet formulation at the dose of 600 mg QD was assessed based on the final population PK model. The analysis indicated a minimal decrease (&lt;10%) of the systemic exposure with the increase of age. Based on these findings, it was concluded that no dosing adjustment is warranted based on age.

## Pediatric population

The safety and efficacy of resminostat in children below 18 years have not been established. No clinical / PK data are available.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmacokinetic interaction

## Resminostat as victim drug

To address resminostat drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential, CYPs's isoforms contribution to resminostat metabolism was tested. In vitro data showed that resminostat is a substrate of CYP2C8, which is expected to be responsible for up to 86% of hepatic metabolism of resminostat, but also of CYP1A2 and 2C19. Based on the obtained results, CYPs are considered the major enzymes contributing to ≥ 25% of the overall elimination of resminostat. Overall, metabolization of resminostat by CYP2C8 is predicted to result in the formation of the metabolites M6, M2, and demethylated M2. Other CYP enzymes are involved in the formation of metabolites M7 and M2.

No clinical study was conducted to assess resminostat potential interactions with CYP2C8, CYP1A2, and CYP2C19 inhibitors as victim drug, or resminostat induction by other drugs.

Two studies were conducted enabling the assessment of PK interactions of resminostat with Sorafenib (Study 4SC-201-1-2009 SHELTER) in patients with hepatocellular carcinoma and the PK interaction of resminostat with FOLFIRI regimen (study 4SC-201-3-2010 SHORE) in patients with k-ras mutated advanced colorectal carcinoma, although these studies were not conducted for formal drug-drug interactions assessment.

These two studies did not show 400 mg sorafenib BID, or FOLFIRI regimen respective co-administration with 600 mg resminostat QD 5 + 9 days cycle affected resminostat PK.

## Resminostat as perpetrator drug

Based on in vitro DDI studies, no clinically relevant interaction with CYPs, and P-gp substrates would be expected.

## Pharmacokinetics using human biomaterials

## N/A

## 2.6.2.2. Pharmacodynamics

## Mechanism of action

Resminostat is a histone deacetylase (HDAC) inhibitor. HDACs catalyze the removal of acetyl groups from acetylated lysine residues in histones, resulting in the modulation of gene expression. HDACs also deacetylate non-histone proteins, such as transcription factors. These enzymes are known to play a central role in the regulation of several cellular properties linked with the development and progression of cancer. By inhibiting these enzymes, resminostat induces changes in the acetylation status of histone and non-histone proteins which results in the transcriptional activation and/or repression of genes that mediate pleiotropic effects such as pathway modulation, cell growth arrest, differentiation, and apoptosis.

## Primary and Secondary pharmacology

The pharmacodynamics of resminostat were evaluated using serial blood sampling in several studies (monotherapy and combination therapy). Data has shown that resminostat dose-dependently inhibits HDAC enzyme activity. Dose levels of 400 mg or higher demonstrated between 80% and 100% enzyme activity inhibition occurring when resminostat achieved peak plasma levels. The inhibition of HDAC enzyme activity by resminostat is transient and returns to baseline activity at 24 h after administration.

Multiple dosing of resminostat increases histone H4 acetylation levels by 1.5 to 2.0-fold at 2 h post-dose compared to baseline (pre-dose) and 2.0 to 4-fold at 2 h post-dose compared to C1D1, regardless of dose level. Median H4 acetylation was restored to baseline levels at 24 h post-dose after resminostat dosing in C1, but from C4D3 onwards some residual hyperacetylation was observed compared to C1D1.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Relationship between plasma concentration and effect and safety

An efficacy exposure-response analysis based on the clinical and the PK data collected in the target patients with SS or MF (RESMAIN study) was conducted. The efficacy endpoint was the primary variable Progression Free Survival (PFS) and the exposures metrics were the observed resminostat concentrations at C3D5 at 2h post dose (Cmax-2h) and the derived AUC. The analysis was performed by evaluating the relationship between the median PFS time and the median levels of exposure (Cmax and AUC) estimated in four groups (placebo, low, intermediate and high eposure).

The median Cmax-2h values with the associated estimated median survival time in each strata is presented in Table 10. The exposure-response relationship between the increase of Cmax-2h and the increase of the median survival is shown in Figure 4. The stratified Kaplan-Meier plot of PFS is presented in Figure 5.
