## 2.6.5.4. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for efficacy

N/A

## 2.6.5.5. Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and meta-analysis)

No comparative analyses with results from other studies are presented as only one pivotal study has been conducted to investigate the efficacy of resminostat as maintenance monotherapy in the proposed therapeutic indication and at the proposed dose and schedule.

## 2.6.5.6. Supportive study(ies)

There was no other supportive study.

## 2.6.6. Discussion on clinical efficacy

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

The pivotal study was a multi-centre, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled phase II trial to evaluate whether resminostat in maintenance setting was able to prolong PFS (primary endpoint) or delay symptom worsening of pruritus (key secondary endpoint) compared with placebo in patients with advanced stage (Stage IIB - IVB) MF or SS with ongoing clinical benefit (CR, PR) or SD after at least one prior systemic therapy or TSEB. Patients were randomized in a ratio of 1:1 to resminostat or matching placebo. Patients on placebo had the option to roll-over to open-label treatment with resminostat and continue on treatment for as long as they derived benefit in the opinion of the investigator or until unacceptable toxicity.

Enrolment was limited to patients with histologically confirmed, advanced stage (≥IIB) MF or SS which can be endorsed, since they represent the population with the highest medical need, and because only patients with advanced disease may have received prior systemic treatments. It is however important to note that although overlaps may exist, MF and SS are distinct entities with different prognosis, which may have an impact on efficacy results and their interpretation. Disease stage was assessed according to the ISCL/EORTC revised staging system for MF/SS (Olsen et al 2007). Patients were also required to present adequate cardiac function and hematological, hepatic and renal function to avoid increased safety risks on study treatment. Anticancer treatment other than resminostat was not allowed. The study included patients that had received at least one systemic treatment according to local institutional standards. The choice of not limiting the possible prior treatments was endorsed by CHMP during the SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016), taking into account that, no recognized standard in the treatment of advanced MF/SS can be identified.

The placebo-controlled study design was considered adequate by CHMP during the SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016) considering the absence of maintenance treatment options. However, for patients experiencing SD or PR with their last systemic treatment, in practice many treatments could be offered as long as the patient did not experience complete response. Indeed, either the treatment was considered effective for the patient and then should have been continued given that many treatments are administered until disease progression, or it was considered insufficient or with unacceptable toxicity and then it should have been modified which is considered a change of line of therapy for greater efficiency and not a maintenance treatment. In summary, patients included in the trial actually correspond to two types of populations, one population justifying maintenance treatment, i.e. patients in complete response for whom no maintenance treatment exists and another population justifying a change in treatment line for unacceptable toxicity or other eligible reasons i.e. patients with partial response or stable disease for whom the choice of placebo as a comparator is questioned given that many treatments could be offered among the range of treatments for advanced MF and SS. However, given that placebo as comparator was accepted in the SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016) and that at this stage, there is no way to address this retrospectively, this issue was not further pursued.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

176 patients with PR (N = 107; 53.2%) or SD (N = 69; 34.3%) at baseline were included in the study. The majority of patients (N = 117, 66.4%) had completed their last prior therapy as planned. This was the case in particular for treatment options with cumulative or irreversible toxicity (e.g. myelosuppression, cardiotoxicity or neuropathy) that are usually given for a defined treatment period or maximum dose, like TSEB radiation therapy, chemotherapy and brentuximab vedotin. The remaining third of patients (N = 59, 33.5%) had discontinued their last prior therapy due to toxicity. To better identify the intended patient population, it was indicated in the SmPC that the treatment should be initiated 2 to 12 weeks after the last systemic therapy has been completed as planned or terminated due to unacceptable toxicity.

The randomisation was stratified by remission status and disease stage. The high heterogeneity of the study population was highlighted as a general concern during scientific advice (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016) and other important baseline characteristics (including but not limited to CTCL subtype and prior therapies) may represent relevant prognostic and/or predictive factors.

The study was a double-blind trial. Each patient was unblinded at the time of progression to determine subsequent treatment, and all remaining patients were unblinded once the main analysis results were available. Due to the gastrointestinal side effects associated with resminostat, the efficacy of blinding was questioned during scientific advice (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016). The applicant clarified at the time that only an average of 2.5 patients per site were expected, which might reduce observer bias. A survey was also conducted to collect the patients and investigators' assumptions regarding the assigned treatment arm, prior to unblinding. In the resminostat arm, 60.0% of both patients and investigators correctly assumed their treatment arm, while 17.0% each incorrectly assumed treatment with placebo. In the placebo arm, 70.3% of the patients and 76.2% of the investigators correctly assumed their treatment arm, with 19.8% and 13.9%, respectively, incorrectly assuming treatment with resminostat. Assumptions were missing for both investigators and patients for 23.0% of patients in the resminostat arm, and for 9.9% of patients in the placebo arm. The Applicant's view that these survey results indicate \"that blinding of the trial treatments was successful\" is not shared. Instead, the large proportion of patients correctly assuming their treatment in both arms (and the small proportion of patients guessing incorrectly) likely reflects an important level of functional unblinding, which may have introduced bias in several efficacy analyses (including the analysis of primary and secondary endpoints).

A composite primary endpoint was PFS per the global response score or death from any cause, whichever occurs first. This is considered an appropriate primary endpoint and GRS is endorsed by the International Society for Cutaneous Lymphomas (ISCL), the United States Cutaneous Lymphoma Consortium (USCLC) and the European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer (EORTC) (Olsen et al., 2011). GRS includes assessment of response based on four components clinically relevant to MF/SS patients: skin, lymph, viscera, and blood. However, it was highlighted during the SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016) that the expected PFS advantage with resminostat may be considered somehow limited, especially taking into account that the median overall survival of the MF (IIb-IVB)/SS patient population may vary between 2 to 5 years (depending on the stage of disease at the time of diagnosis) and that disease-specific survival shows an even larger spreading (2 to &gt;10 years, see. i.e. Horwiz et al., Journal of the National Comprehensive Cancer Network, 2008). In recommendations endorsed by ISCL, USCLC and EORTC (Olsen et al., 2011) it was stressed that due to the importance of skin response in MF/SS, both skin and global response should be reported in clinical trials to ensure that skin response which independently affects prognosis and quality of life is not lost within the global score. mSWAT was not included as a secondary endpoint in the pivotal clinical trial; a post hoc summary of the changes of mSWAT scores was provided, and patient reported outcomes including VAS itching score was evaluated, which is considered sufficient to assess skin response.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall survival was included as a secondary endpoint but was not included in the multiplicity adjustment strategy and was not alfa controlled. As median overall survival is approximately 5 years in patients with advanced CTCL, the applicant considered not feasible to include survival as a primary endpoint. This can be supported but still requires that no detrimental effect is observed on survival. PFS2, PFS3, and TTNT were included as secondary endpoints, which are considered particularly important for the evaluation of long-term efficacy and the actual extent of the clinical benefit of resminostat maintenance, as indicated in EMA SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016), however, TTNT is also influenced by cross-over study design. Secondary endpoints of confirmed ORR, DOR, and TTP are standard in oncology and thus acceptable. HRQoL assessed using FACT-G, Skindex-29, and VAS itching score is also considered clinically important for patients with MF/SS, and thus acceptable.

The study was 80% powered only to test a hypothesis for the primary endpoint of PFS, where the key secondary endpoint (TTSW) is tested using a hierarchical approach if the null hypothesis for the primary endpoint is rejected. No adjustments for multiple tests were applied for subsequent secondary endpoint analyses, and the results for these endpoints, therefore, can only be considered as descriptive. The results for analysis of TTSW (pruritus) would only be considered confirmative, if the analysis of the primary endpoint showed a significant result (p-value ≤ 0.05). This hierarchical approach keeps the global significance level to 0.05 two-sided and required no further adjustment.

There was no planned adjustment for multiplicity for other secondary endpoints.

In Resmain study, progressive disease was assessed according to the definition of the consensus statement of the International Society for Cutaneous Lymphomas, the US Cutaneous Lymphoma Consortium and the EORTC Cutaneous Lymphoma Task Force using the proposed global response score comprising of the assessment of tumour response in skin, lymph nodes, viscera and blood (Olsen 2011). This was endorsed during the SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016) since the proposed global response score (GRS) combining responses in skin, lymph nodes, blood, and viscera is considered, a standard efficacy measure in trials involving MF/SS.

According to inclusion criteria, the diagnosis of MF/SS was confirmed histologically. Initially, biopsy of one lesion was mandatory at the start of the trial and after PD. The performance of skin biopsies was terminated in Protocol Version 8.0. Additionally, PD or relapse of the disease in the lymph nodes should have been histologically proven. The protocol amendment was implemented on December 21, 2022 and according to the date of main analysis, all patients should have been randomised, therefore it is considered that this amendment will have minor influence on eligibility of study participants, as diagnosis should be confirmed in a skin biopsy before trial initiation. For evaluation of response in skin according to Olsen et. al. (2011) recommendations a skin biopsy should be performed of a representative area of the skin if there is any question of residual disease, and is more important for differentiating CR vs PR, however, as there were only few patients with CR, it is considered that this amendment have minor influence on evaluation of response. If a bone marrow biopsy was performed at baseline and was determined to unequivocally be indicative of lymphomatous involvement, then to confirm a global CR, a repeat bone marrow biopsy must show no residual disease or the response should be considered PR only; however, bone marrow biopsy was not routinely performed in every participant. According to Olsen et al. (2011) bone marrow involvement in MF/SS can be considered to be an extension of blood involvement and not visceral disease, and therefore bone marrow aspirate biopsies are not considered obligatory for evaluation or response assessment. Based on this the applicant's approach is not concerning.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The use of the mSWAT instrument to assess skin response in trials with a mixed MF and SS population was endorsed by CHMP during the SA since it allows to simultaneously evaluate both erythrodermia and any present tumour. The every 4 weeks' assessment schedule was also acceptable. However, a modification of the standard assessment was introduced, to allow for PD assessment in patients entering the study with a mSWAT of 0 (CR in skin): a mSWAT of at least 20 and a ≥ 25% increase in skin disease from baseline was considered PD instead of: a ≥ 25% increase in skin disease from baseline. Therefore, for a patient who entered the study with a CR (mSWAT of 0) or with a very good PR to prior therapy (mSWAT below 16), any increase in mSWAT to less than 20 and without new tumours should not be considered progressive disease, but SD in skin. This is acknowledged since in this study, patients were enrolled with a very low mSWAT, and that this would mean that a very small increase (25%) in skin disease would already meet the criteria for disease progression. However, a sensitivity analysis of PFS using the current response criteria of the 2011 ISCL/USCLC/EORTC consensus statement in skin disease, without the modification related to mSWAT value at baseline was provided. The applicant presented steps that were taken to reduce inter-observer variability in assessing skin lesions. These steps were overall reassuring. It was emphasized that the mSWAT assessment should be performed by the same investigator at all-time points to eliminate inter-observer variability for a given patient.

Centralised flow cytometry identification of circulating neoplastic lymphocytes (either CD4+CD26- or CD4+CD7-) is proposed to assess response in peripheral blood. This was endorsed, by CHMP although it was advised, to rely on the absolute number of pathologic lymphocytes rather than on binary positive/negative evaluations given that results based only on immunophenotype can be sometimes misleading due to the possible presence of non-neoplastic CD4+CD26-CD7-.

The use of computed tomography (CT) for assessment of lymph nodes and visceral involvement and the proposed assessment schedule were acceptable by CHMP. CT scan were performed at baseline and repeated every 12 weeks during the study.

Complete response assessments as per GRS (mSWAT, lymph nodes, viscera, blood) were to be performed at start of treatment and then once every 84 days (± 7 days) until blinded EOT. Intermediate response assessments (IRA), i.e., mSWAT and blood only, were to be performed at the start of treatment and then once every 28 days (± 7 days) until blinded EOT. If one of two tested compartments was PD, then a GRS response of PD was assigned. However, an improvement in GRS response (CR or PR) or stable in GRS response (SD) could only be assigned with a complete GRS assessment (i.e., lymph nodes and viscera assessed by CT in addition to mSWAT and blood analysis).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

HrQoL was determined using the validated questionnaires FACT-G, skindex-29 and visual analogue scale (VAS). FACT-G is a 27-item questionnaire designed to measure physical, social, emotional, and functional well-being in patients undergoing cancer therapy. Skindex-29 is a dermatology-specific questionnaire to measure the effects of skin disease on patients' QoL. The VAS is a psychometric response scale to quantify the severity of itching. Overall, the proposed HRQoL instruments are considered sufficiently validated and adequate to capture relevant changes in patient reported outcomes as reported in SA (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016).

The primary analysis set for PFS (ITT population) includes all randomized patients. A per protocol population has also been defined for sensitivity analysis. A TTSW (pruritus) population has been used for the key secondary population, restricting the analysis to randomised patients with a VAS itching score ≤ 7 at baseline, and excluding patients without a VAS itching score at baseline. The intent to only include patients at risk of worsening can be understood, but in the absence of stratification by VAS itching score may have led to some imbalance across treatment arms. Nevertheless, given the lack of treatment effect observed for the TTSW (pruritus) key secondary endpoint, and acknowledging the sensitivity analysis in the ITT population, no related questions were raised.

The censoring rules defined for PFS are not in line with EMA guidance (EMA/CHMP/27994/2008/Rev.1). Indeed, a treatment policy strategy would have been preferred for the primary analysis, i.e. with no censoring applied prior to non-study cancer treatment nor in case of at least two consecutive incomplete / missing global response assessments (GRAs). As requested, the Applicant provided an alternative PFS analysis where censoring rules were modified with no censoring applied to non-study cancer treatment nor in case of at least two consecutive incomplete / missing GRAs. The results were consistent with the primary PFS results. However, there are limitations with this additional analysis which prevent a meaningful interpretation, in particular the inconsistency in response assessment between the blinded period and the follow-up period. It is unfortunate that GRA was not continuously collected after treatment discontinuation until at least the first PD.

Moreover, the treatment policy approach requires for the primary analysis of PFS that outcome data should be collected according to the intended schedule of assessment and the date of progression should be assigned based on the time of the first evidence of objective progression regardless of intercurrent events (violations, discontinuation of study drug or change of therapy) for all randomised patients. Patients who discontinued for any reason other than PD were considered dropouts and last available evaluation was used for endpoint evaluation. These patients were included in the survival follow-up. As the PFS data for the primary endpoint was not available after the dropout, this is not in accordance with the treatment policy approach.

The presence of functional unblinding based on the results of the blinding survey is of concern. The impact of several scenarios involving the unblinding of either the patient or the investigator were discussed by the Applicant, with a focus on the impact on treatment withdrawal. However, not only treatment withdrawal may be affected by functional unblinding, but also the actual measurements themselves (for GRS components that are investigator-assessed).

The Applicant clarified that an independent central review of mSWAT assessment is not feasible based on photographs. Indeed, it would often be impossible to differentiate between patches and plaques, or skin areas with inflammatory changes from erythhrodermic skin, based on photographs only. Skin being the leading disease compartment with 82% of disease events occurring in skin, a potential central review of other components (LN and viscera) would not substantially change the reliability of the primary endpoint. The impossibility to perform a blinded central review prevents a meaningful assessment of the potential bias introduced by functional unblinding.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The key secondary endpoint TTSW (pruritus) may also be subject to informative censoring for the same reasons. Due to the lack of demonstration of a treatment effect based on the current set of statistical analyses, the issue was not pursued for TTSW.

Based on the current OS results, very few patients were lost to follow up for survival status (1 in resminostat group, 2 in placebo), thereby indicating negligible impact of informative censoring. However, the analysis of OS is confounded by the crossover design.

The endpoint TTNT is confounded by the crossover design. Indeed, the definition of next treatment includes crossover, which can only be an option for patients in the placebo arm. The Applicant clarified that 72 (84.7%) of TTNT events in the placebo arm correspond to patients crossing over to resminostat. This confirms that TTNT is very likely biased. Indeed, crossover can only be an option for patients in the placebo arm. Whether as many patients would have initiated a subsequent therapy in the absence of crossover, and whether these subsequent therapies would have been initiated at the same time point, is speculative and unlikely.

HrQoL endpoints (VAS, FACT-G, Skindex-29) and the post-hoc analysis of changes in mSWAT score were assessed using a mixed model that assumes the linearity of measurements over time. This is a rather strong assumption, which has not been discussed. It is also noted that HrQoL and mSWAT score analyses are not adjusted for the randomisation stratification factors. .

It is noted that some emphasis is given in the study report and in the summary documents to the post-hoc analysis of PFS from start of last prior systemic therapy to first PD. However, the use of last prior systemic therapy as a starting point, instead of randomisation, is subject to immortal time bias which has not been accounted for. This has likely resulted in a distortion of the observed treatment effect.

The main changes to planned analyses were incorporated in protocol amendments 4.0, 6.0 and 7.0, which were subsequently also included in the relevant SAP versions. The sample size, in terms of number of patients to be enrolled, was increased in protocol amendments 6.0 and 7.0. The increase was from 150 to 190 patients with protocol version 6, then from 190 to 200 patients with protocol version 7, to account for larger anticipated drop-out rates, from 10% to 30%, and then from 30% to 33% respectively. It is acknowledged that the targeted number of events (n=125) remained the same across all protocol versions, as well as all other sample size assumptions. The main impact of these modifications is therefore the expected lower maturity. The resulting PFS analysis may also be driven by earlier events than initially expected. Another important concern related to the higher drop-out rate is the impact of informative censoring on the PFS primary analysis, as already discussed above.

A hierarchical testing procedure was followed, with the analysis of TTSW (pruritus) only being performed if the PFS primary analysis showed a significant result at the two-sided 0.05 alpha level. There was no multiplicity adjustment for other secondary endpoints, which should therefore be considered of exploratory nature.

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

A total of 234 patients (including 11 patients who were re-screened and thus each counted twice) were screened for trial participation at 55 active study sites across Europe and Japan. A total of 201 patients were randomized to either resminostat (100) or placebo (101), with eligibility criterion not met being the main reason for screening failure (18/33).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As of 10 March 2023, 92 subjects (92%) in the resminostat arm had discontinued their treatment compared to 93 subjects (92%) in the placebo arm. The most commonly reported reasons for treatment discontinuation were PD, which was more common in the placebo arm (69%) than in the resminostat arm (49%); patient withdrawal which was more common in the resminostat arm (25%) than in the placebo arm (5%) and AEs, which was more common in the resminostat arm (8%) than in the placebo arm (5%). Until the DCO date (10 March 2023) for the primary analysis, 72 patients randomized to placebo rolled over to resminostat. A total of 30 patients (14.9%) have withdrawn the consent during the course of the study, 25 of these patients were treated in the resminostat group and 5 in the placebo group. The most frequent reason for this imbalance was tolerability complaints (19 patients in resminostat vs 0 in placebo) mainly related to gastrointestinal disorders like nausea (mainly grade 1 or 2), dysgeusia and fatigue.

There were 7 amendments to the protocol. The main changes were the addition of evaluation of changes in the VAS itching score, modification of the allowed window between completion of prior treatment and randomization (from 8 weeks to 12 weeks), modification of exclusion criterion #8 (history of prior cancers), to add systemic steroids as anti-itching medication to the list of prohibited medication, and to allow reescalation of the dose to 600 mg/day if the dose was reduced due to nausea, vomiting or similar gastrointestinal events.

Minor protocol deviations were reported in 187 patients (93.0%). Major protocol deviations were highly reported in the study; 80% in resminostat arm and 74% in placebo arm. Major PD were related to procedures not done, or outside time window, deviations in incl./excl. criteria, administration of prohibited medication, ICF issues, laboratory issues (samples lost or received late, not done), study drug issues (potential/actual blind broken, dosing non-compliance, incorrect medication/randomization) and visit schedule issues (incomplete, not done, outside time window).

A total of 10 patients (10.0%) and 17 patients (16.8%) in the resminostat and placebo arm respectively were excluded from the PP population. This was due to the following reasons: determination of PD not fulfilling the PD criteria, absence of assessment for the ECG parameter of QTc (leading to early discontinuation), missing first 2 post-baseline GRS assessments, violation of eligibility criteria, missing GRS before PD and wrong subject medication given.

The applicant indicated that the study 4SC-201 was conducted in accordance with local and/or national regulations, the ICH GCP guideline and the ethical principles of Declaration of Helsinki and applicable regulatory requirements, and informed consent was obtained from participants. Several GCP audits were conducted on behalf of the sponsor at the trial sites, including remote audits due to the COVID-19 pandemic, which comprised the EMA guidance on the management of clinical trials during the COVID-19 pandemic and the EMA guidance on remote GCP inspections during the COVID-19 pandemic. Three regulatory inspections were conducted at the trial sites (two in Germany and one in Switzerland). It was considered that the clinical trial was conducted in accordance with GCP and review of the provided CAPA plan has shown that the measures are sufficient and suitable to eliminate the deficiencies and avoid them in future studies. Overall, the study included a broad range of patients with advanced-stage MF or SS who received a large variety of prior systemic therapies, resembling the real world CTCL patient population. The last prior systemic therapy was bexarotene/other retinoids (23%), chemotherapy (23.4%), brentuximab vedotin (15%), TSEB (12%) and ECP (6%).

The demographic characteristics were median age of 64 years (30 to 87 years) with 45% of patients' ≥ 65 years. 61% of patients were male, 39% were female. Disease stage at baseline was IIB-IVA1 in 83% (58% stage IIB, 6% stage IIIA, 6% stage IIIB, 13% stage IVA1) and IVA2-IVB in 17% of patients (13% stage IVA2, 5% stage IVB). At baseline 12.4% of patients had CR, 53.2% of patients had PR and 34.3% of patients had SD. Patients were slightly younger than reported in the literature and therefore might have better prognosis. Groups were similar in terms of median age, ECOG performance, CTCL subtype and anti-itching treatment at baseline. Resminostat group had lower median weight (74.8 vs 80 kg), slightly shorter duration of the disease and fewer patients with more advanced - IVA1 un IVB stages compared to placebo. In terms of remission status, more patients in resminostat group had CR and SD while placebo group had more patients with PR. More patients in resminostat group had large cell transformation (n=24; 24%) vs placebo (n=13; 12.9%) which is associated with more aggressive disease course. Although there are several discrepancies between the groups it is difficult to assess whether any of them could introduce a systematic bias in the results. There were more women in the resminostat group (n=47; 47%) compared to placebo group (n=31; 30.7%). Literature data suggest that being women is independently associated with better outcomes in the MF diagnosis (https://www.jaad.org/article/S0190-9622(21)00171-7/fulltext). The applicant argues that although women are reported to have better outcomes there are still conflicting results regarding the role of gender as a prognostic factor when accounting for disease characteristics and treatment. In addition, the applicant considers that based on the pharmacological mechanism of resminostat as HDAC inhibitor possible gender-specific differences are considered unlikely. The Applicant suggests that observed changes notably lower PFS for men subgroup compared to women may be driven by increased body weight in male participants as the PK data indicate that increased body weight is associated with decreased exposure.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All patients had received previous systemic therapy with up to 21 treatment lines (mean: 4.1; median: 3.0). As expected, due to the lack of established treatment recommendations, patients had been treated with a variety of different previous therapies.

Based on subgroup analysis, it seems that patients with less than 3 prior lines of therapies retrieve better efficacy with resminostat (PFS of 26.9 months with resminostat vs 4.2 months with placebo (HR 0.464, 95% CI: 0.260, 0.829)) than patients with extensive prior line of therapies (more than 3 prior therapy) (PFS of 6.0 months vs 4.6 months with placebo (HR 0.9, 95% CI: 0.522, 1.554)). Regarding the high mPFS reported in the resminostat arm (26.9 months) of the subgroup of patients with 1, 2 or 3 prior therapy, this seems overestimated by the KM curves due to the late extensive censoring.

In total, nearly half of the patients (47.8%) had at least one prior disease. SOCs were distributed similarly between the resminostat and placebo arms for the most common SOCs (20.0% versus 26.7% for surgical and medical procedures, 14.0% versus 11.9% for neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified, and 8.0% versus 12.9% for infections and infestations). The majority of patients (87.6%) had at least one concomitant condition.

Almost all patients (96.5%) received concomitant medications during study. However, some imbalances were reported with higher use of some classes of drug in resminostat arm, reflecting the need to manage safety risks of resminostat. This particularly concerns drugs for peptic ulcer and gastrooesophageal reflux disease (GORD), emollients and protectives, propulsives, anti-emetics and anti-nauseants to manage gastrointestinal side effects, beta-lactam and other anti-baterials for the management of infections related to treatment and analgesics, antipyretics, and corticosteroids for systemic use mainly to manage pain and nausea or vomiting.

Patients in the resminostat arm received slightly more corticosteroids than patients in the placebo arm (51 vs. 45.5%), more antihistamines (45 vs 42.6%), more systemic corticosteroids (24 vs 14.9%). Emollients and protectives 31 (31.0%) in resminostat group vs 20 (19.8%) in placebo and opioids 16 (16.0%) in resminostat group vs 9 (8.9%) in placebo group. These discrepancies may confound study results on pruritis improvement in favour of resminostat group.

The main analysis showed a statistically significant improvement in PFS for resminostat compared with placebo, with a median PFS of 8.3 months versus 4.2 months, respectively, and an HR of 0.623 in favor of treatment with resminostat in the overall patient population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The stability of study results was questioned based on the sensitivity analysis where discontinuation due to toxicity or start of new treatment before PD were considered as an event. Indeed, this sensitivity analysis showed a smaller effect size and did not reach nominal statistical significance. Given the safety profile of resminostat and the imbalance observed in treatment withdrawals for toxicity, it may be reasonable to consider discontinuations due to toxicity or new treatments before PD as unfavourable outcomes - and therefore a sensible assumption to consider them as events in this alternative PFS analysis.

It was confirmed that the vast majority of the PFS censoring is non-administrative and indicates informative censoring. As suspected, it is also apparent that withdrawals due to tolerability issues or drug-related adverse events were the main non-administrative reason for PFS censoring in the resminostat treatment group. This pattern was not observed in the placebo arm. Instead, several censored measurements in the placebo arm (more than in the resminostat arm) were due to physician decisions based on increases in mSWAT not fulfilling PD criteria, and a few others due to treatment withdrawal for lack of efficacy / patient's assumption to be on placebo.

Based on these censoring patterns, the Applicant concludes that post-censoring hazards are likely increased in both treatment groups, but that this increase is probably less pronounced in the placebo arm, so that the treatment effect in PFS might actually be larger than in the primary analysis results. This is based on the Applicant's assumption that withdrawals due to tolerability are unrelated to PFS outcomes, whereas withdrawals related to increases in mSWAT not fulfilling PD criteria would be indicative of a negative outcome. This interpretation is not agreed.

Firstly, and as further discussed above, it seems reasonable to assume that treatment withdrawals due to tolerability issues are associated with more negative outcomes, because of the adverse effect itself and/or the delay it may cause to further treatment. Given the very large imbalance in withdrawals because of tolerability or drug-related side effects (n=19 with resminostat vs n=2 with placebo), this is likely to result in a more pronounced increase in post-censoring hazard in the resminostat group.

Moreover, although the larger proportion of censoring in the placebo group due to increases in mSWAT not fulfilling PD might be associated with a higher risk of post-censoring hazard to some degree, it is unclear whether such increase is truly meaningful. Indeed, some of the increases observed, although describing a trend towards disease worsening, do not seem close to reaching PD criteria. Moreover, such decision to withdraw from treatment early based on increases that do not reach the protocol criteria is likely influenced by the extent of functional unblinding.

Therefore, a post-censoring hazard higher in the resminostat arm than in the placebo arm for nonadministratively censored patients is a plausible assumption. In this context, the results of the tipping point analysis are of concern. Indeed, most investigated scenarios with higher multiplicative increases in the resminostat arm than in the placebo arm result in loss of statistical significance, with smaller effect sizes than in the primary analysis. For instance, increasing the post-censoring hazard for the resmonistat group by only a factor of 1.25 leads to the loss of significance in the log-rank test (p=0.06).

The robustness of the PFS analysis could not be shown in the single pivotal trial. This is due to the extent of informative censoring, the large imbalance in withdrawals due to toxicity and the potential impact of functional unblinding. The effect of resminostat on PFS may be over-estimated to the point where the effect is not statistically significant. Moreover, there is no treatment difference in OS, and the absence of treatment effect persists after adjustment for crossover (with sensitivity analyses indicating a potential detriment).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Importantly, given the large confidence limits, the OS data cannot rule out a substantial detriment on survival. There is also no clear support from other secondary endpoints. Consequently, the efficacy of resminostat was not considered established.

TTP and TTNT results were in favour of resminostat, with a median TTP of 8.3 months on resminostat and 4.2 months on placebo (HR of 0.623) and a median TTNT of 8.8 months on resminostat and 4.2 months on placebo (HR of 0.594). The statistical significance claim of these endpoints is not supported given that results are purely exploratory due to absence of multiplicity adjustment. Furthermore, patient drop-out rate was high with more premature withdrawals in the resminostat group which raises a concern of informative censoring with an imbalance between treatment arms.

Among patients with baseline remission status of PR or SD, ORR rate was 17.4% (10.104, 27.131) and 10% [4.676, 18.136] in resminostat and placebo arm, respectively with similar median DOR. These results should be interpreted with caution given their exploratory nature, and the risk of bias due to elimination of a subpopulation (patients with CR at baseline). The relevance of ORR in a maintenance therapy setting is limited, since patients were already in remission (CR or PR) or at least in SD when starting with maintenance treatment. Further, the prior therapy may have contributed to the responses observed during treatment with resminostat or placebo.

The results for OS at the final analysis (data cut off: 08 August 2024) were similar to those at the main analysis and showed no significant difference between the treatment arms (p-value 0.940, HR 0.983 (0.606, 1.590)). Two analyses were performed by the Applicant to investigate the effect of crossover on OS results: censoring at time of crossover (planned by SAP), and a RPSFTM analysis (post-hoc, one of several analyses mentioned in EMA/845963/2018 to explore the effect of crossover on OS). These analyses did not provide reassurance on the OS data. Indeed, analyses adjusting for crossover consistently showed no treatment effect on OS, and even indicated a potential detriment.

Analyses of PFS2 (p-value 0.156, HR 0.758 (0.516, 1.113)), and PFS3 (p-value 0.921, HR 1.021 (0.674, 1.547)) at the final analysis time point (data cut off: 08 August 2024) do not indicate a statistically significant difference between treatment arms and do not provide reassurance on long-term effect of treatment.

The analysis of the key secondary endpoint TTSW pruritus in the TTSW population including the sensitivity analysis in the ITT population and in a population including only patients with all scheduled assessments of symptom (pruritus) worsening do not allow to retrieve any conclusion about the potential effect of resminostat on pruritis symptoms. Within the TTSW population (n = 154), 14 TTSW pruritus events (19.4% of patients) were observed on resminostat and 11 events (13.4% of patients) on placebo. The estimation of median TTSW was not possible due to the limited number of events. Limited information on TTSW pruritus is considered as an important limitation in B/R analysis of resminostat.

In summary, no benefit of resminostat on itching based on results of changes from baseline in VAS itching could be demonstrated. The mean scores of itching based on VAS did not change over time (mean values about 2-4) in both arms and there was no clinically significant difference in the observed changes from baseline over time for resminostat versus placebo using a MMRM. In addition, there were high number of missing assessments over time.

No benefit of resminostat on FACT-G scores were reported over time, contrariwise, results were in favour of placebo. Also, the number of patients with available FACT-G scores decreased over time. Precisely worsening in the \"physical well-being\" domain was observed with resminostat but remained stable with placebo. Furthermore, the observed changes from baseline in FACT-G scores for resminostat versus placebo based on the ITT population using a linear MMRM were in favor of placebo.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There was no benefit of resminostat on Skindex-29 questionnaire. The difference in the observed changes from baseline over time for resminostat versus placebo was slight and not clinically significant. The requirement of anti-itching treatment or doses change of anti-itching treatment during the study was similar in both treatment arms and comprised predominantly corticosteroids and antihistamines for systemic use, which is in line with the results of the VAS itching scale where no benefit of treatment on itching symptoms was reported.

In summary, QoL endpoints did not show improvement in the resminostat group. Some analysis suggests worsening in resminostat group compared to placebo.

A post-analysis of PFS from start of last prior systemic therapy to first occurrence of PD (= total PFS) in the ITT population based on the same events as described for the primary endpoint of PFS, showed a median total PFS of 24.3 months for resminostat compared with 14.9 months for placebo with an HR of 0.745 in favor of resminostat. However, the use of last prior systemic therapy as a starting point, instead of randomisation, is subject to immortal time bias which has not been accounted for. This has likely resulted in a distortion of the observed treatment effect.

The difference in median PFS between the treatment arms for the subgroup of SS patients was smaller (4.2 months for placebo vs 5.6 months for resminostat) than for the subgroup of MF patients (4.2 months for placebo vs 9.2 months for resminostat) mainly due to the lower mPFS reported in the resminostat arm for SS group. The Applicant acknowledges that data concerning the efficacy of resminostat in SS is limited and agrees that PFS results are neither clinically or statistically significant. OS data in this subgroup are uninterpretable given the very low number of death events. The applicant provided a detailed analysis of prognostic factors that favouring placebo to justify the reported results in this subgroup of patients. The imbalance in disease characteristics and prognostic factors could possibly explain the reported limited results in PFS. However, it cannot be excluded that these results may as well translate an absence of a clinical effect in SS a different entity of CTCL than MF with more aggressive disease course. The extent of efficacy of resminostat in SS could not be assessed and there is no retrospective solution to this issue.

The results of patients who switched to rollover treatment with open-label resminostat due to PD are difficult to interpret given that patients who crossed-over do not correspond to the target population. These are relapsed/progressive patient population and have received resminostat as a treatment alternative after PD. Whereas resminostat targets patients that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy.

Patients with TSEB as the last systemic therapy prior to trial entry had a median PFS of 9.7 months, on resminostat versus 39.8 months for placebo (HR 1.520 (0.443, 5.219)). The number of events was very small and survival curves intersect rendering interpretation of survival data in this subgroup very difficult.

The applicant presented PFS subgroup analyses and corresponding Kaplan-Meier curves for subgroups by prior remission status (CR vs. PR vs. SD) and for combined subgroups (CR vs. PR/SD) as requested. Overall, results in these subgroups are consistent with primary analysis.

Consistently with PFS subgroup analysis, the increase in median TTP on resminostat was limited in patients with SS rather inexistent given the overlapping CIs (5.6 months [1.9, 23.1] vs 4.2 months [1.6, 31.4] HR 0.858 [0.294, 2.507]) but was consistent to the primary analysis for patients with MF, subgroup of patients based on the response status to prior therapy and the disease stage.

A post-hoc subgroup analysis of PFS and TTNT was performed for each of the disease stages IIB, III and IV

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

and for the disease stages III and IV combined. Although limitations related to the small sample size in some subpopulations, PFS and TTNT results in all disease stage subgroups were overall consistent with the results reported in ITT population. The treatment effect using a modified FACT-G total score, with the physical wellbeing question \"I have nausea\" set to \"not at all\" for all patients to test the impact of nausea, was -2.9463 [95% CI: -6.4123, 0.5197], i.e., still in favor of placebo, but smaller than the effect of the original FACT-G total score (-3.736 [-7.310, -0.162]).

## 2.6.7. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The efficacy of resminostat was assessed in one single pivotal phase 2 study in patients with advanced-stage CTCL (MF or SS) that had achieved disease control or clinical response (SD, PR and CR) with previous systemic therapy or TSEB. PFS results were statistically significant (4 months of PFS gain) but it was not possible to interpret given the lack of robustness of results due to various methodological concerns, the absence of support from other secondary endpoints considered clinically relevant (Qol and pruritis improvement) and OS sensitivity analyses indicating a potential detriment. The population of the study is too heterogeneous in terms of class of response, number of prior therapies, and type of CTCL and inconsistencies in PFS results were reported in many subpopulations (particularly SS) reflecting the heterogeneity of the trial population. These uncertainties do not allow to conclude on the efficacy of resminostat.

For the randomized phase, a treatment-emergent AE was defined as any event that started, or any preexisting AE that worsened in severity, on or after the date of first study treatment administration, until the day prior to the start of rollover administration for placebo patients that rolled over, or until 30 days after the last dose for all other patients. For the rollover phase, AEs were defined as treatment-emergent from the date of the first study treatment administration in the rollover period, until 30 days after the last dose. AEs that started on or after the date of first study treatment dose in the randomized phase, but before the start of the rollover phase, were assigned as treatment-emergent AEs for the treatment received in the randomized phase but were considered non-treatment-emergent for the rollover phase [4SC-201-6-2015, 16.1.9].

For the randomized phase, a treatment-emergent AE was defined as any event that started, or any preexisting AE that worsened in severity, on or after the date of first study treatment administration, until the day prior to the start of rollover administration for placebo patients that rolled over, or until 30 days after the last dose for all other patients. TEAEs by severity Treatment emergent AEs of Grade 3 severity were more frequently reported in the resminostat group during the randomized phase (34% of patients) and in patients in the rollover phase (35%) than in the placebo group during the randomized phase (13% of patients); events of Grade 4 severity were reported in 4% of patients in the resminostat group (randomized phase), 3% of patients in the rollover phase, and 2% of patients in the placebo group (randomized phase). No events of Grade 5 severity were reported.

AE: adverse event; N: number of patients randomized; n: number of patients affected; PT: preferred term; SOC: system organ class. Source: Table 14.3.1.4.1.

During the rollover phase, events of Grade 3 severity reported for more than one patient (1%) were lymphocyte count decreased (three patients, 4%), and vomiting, decreased appetite, and squamous cell carcinoma (two patients, 3%, each); Grade 4 myocardial infarction and supraventricular tachycardia were reported for one patient (1%) each.

## Serious adverse events, deaths, and other significant events

## AESI

None.

## Deaths

No deaths were reported during study treatment in Study 4SC 201 6 2015 (RESMAIN). There were no treatment-related deaths during Studies 7341/EM-001 (FIM) or 4SC-201-2-2009 (SAPHIRE), or in the 600 mg resminostat monotherapy group in Study 4SC-201-1-2009 (SHELTER), and no deaths occurred within 30 days of the last dose of resminostat in Study YHI-1001-ST-01. Until data cut-off in Study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) (10 March 2023), 28 patients in the resminostat group and 29 patients in the placebo group, including those subsequently treated with resminostat in the rollover phase, had died. In Study 7341/EM-001 (FIM), 2 patients died within 30 days of study termination. In Study 4SC 201 2 2009 (SAPHIRE), 1 death occurred during the treatment period and 24 deaths occurred during the follow-up period; all except one (unknown cause) were related to disease progression. In the 600 mg resminostat monotherapy group in Study 4SC 201 1 2009 (SHELTER), there were 17 deaths which were mostly due to disease progression/underlying disease (due to unknown cause and myocardial infarction in 1 patient each).

## Deaths

No deaths were reported during study treatment in Study 4SC 201 6 2015 (RESMAIN). There were no treatment-related deaths during Studies 7341/EM-001 (FIM) or 4SC-201-2-2009 (SAPHIRE), or in the 600 mg resminostat monotherapy group in Study 4SC-201-1-2009 (SHELTER), and no deaths occurred within 30 days of the last dose of resminostat in Study YHI-1001-ST-01. Until data cut-off in Study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) (10 March 2023), 28 patients in the resminostat group and 29 patients in the placebo group, including those subsequently treated with resminostat in the rollover phase, had died. In Study 7341/EM-001 (FIM), 2 patients died within 30 days of study termination. In Study 4SC 201 2 2009 (SAPHIRE), 1 death occurred during the treatment period and 24 deaths occurred during the follow-up period; all except one (unknown cause) were related to disease progression. In the 600 mg resminostat monotherapy group in Study 4SC 201 1 2009 (SHELTER), there were 17 deaths which were mostly due to disease progression/underlying disease (due to unknown cause and myocardial infarction in 1 patient each).

## 2.6.8.5. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

N/A

## 2.6.8.6. Safety in special populations

## Age

Resminostat is not indicated for use in the paediatric population due to missing data from clinical trials regarding efficacy and safety in that group. Furthermore, the overwhelming majority of paediatric patients present with early-stage disease (Stages IA, IB, IIA), and in contrast to adults, progression to more advanced stages, such as tumour stage, erythrodermic MF, and lymph node, blood or visceral involvement is extremely rare or even not reported (Ferenci 2016, Delost 2019, Kothari 2022).

45% (256/563) of the subjects who were enrolled in the clinical studies and received resminostat were older than 64 years. Therefore, there was a high percentage of elderly patients in the clinical study program. The efficacy and safety of resminostat has been established in subjects in the age range of 19 to 87 years.

The results of the PopPK covariate analysis showed that the volume of distribution of resminostat was significantly affected by age. This had minimal impact on exposure and was therefore assessed as clinically not relevant.

## Hepatic and Renal Impairment

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No targeted studies have been performed in patients with renal disorders. In patients with renal impairment, efficacy and safety of resminostat was not different compared to patients with normal renal function. In a population PK study data from 228 patients from 6 clinical trials were included and investigated for covariates with influence on the PK parameters of resminostat. Of these patients, 32.9% (75) had mild and 7.9% (18) had moderate renal impairment according to approximated glomerular filtration rate (GFR) assessed by using the Cockcroft Gault formula. Population PK analysis showed that renal function has no influence on the PK of resminostat.

Thus, there is currently no evidence for increased risk for patients with mild to moderate renal impairment.

No targeted studies have been performed in patients with hepatic impairment. Four studies have been performed in patients with hepatic disorders (hepatocellular carcinoma, biliary tract cancer). However, only 12 of the 227 patients (5.3%) showed a moderately impaired liver function (Child-Pugh B7 &amp; B8). It should be noted that resminostat is metabolized by several Cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes, especially CYP2C8, that are not exclusively expressed in the liver but also present in other organs. Thus, in the case of hepatic impairment, a compensatory extrahepatic metabolic capacity might be present.

| AE leading to changes in trial treatment      | 51 (51.0)               | 12 (11.9)           | 63 (31.3)         |
| AE leading to dose reduction                  | 32 (32.0)               | 1 (1.0)             | 34 (16.9)         |

AE: adverse event; N: number of patients randomized; n: number of patients affected; PT: preferred term; SOC: system organ class.

During the randomized phase, AEs leading to changes in study treatment were reported more frequently in the resminostat group than in the placebo group (51 patients (51%) versus 12 patients (12%). In the resminostat group, there were dose reductions (from 600 mg to 400 mg resminostat) in 32 patients (32%), dose delays in 27 patients (27%), and treatment interruptions in 21 patients (21%); related events led to dose reductions in 31 patients (31%), dose delays in 16 patients (16%), and treatment interruptions in 16 patients (16%). In the placebo group, there were dose reductions in 2 patients (2%), dose delays in

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

7 patients (7%), and treatment interruptions in 7 patients (7%); related events led to dose reductions in 1 patient (1%), dose delays in 2 patients (2%), and treatment interruptions in 2 patients (2%).

Permanent discontinuation of resminostat due to AEs was reported in eight patients (8%) during the randomized phase. Related AEs led to withdrawal of resminostat in seven patients (7%); by patient and severity these were Grade 1 nausea, vomiting, and weight decreased, Grade 3 vomiting, Grade 2 dermatitis allergic, Grade 3 myocardial ischaemia, Grade 3 pulmonary embolism, Grade 2 nausea, and Grade 2 fatigue. Events resolved in six patients and outcome was unknown for events in one patient. One event assessed as not related (dementia Alzheimer's type) led to permanent discontinuation of study treatment in a patient in the resminostat group. A possibly related event of lymphopenia of Grade 4 severity led to permanent discontinuation of study treatment in a patient in the placebo group; the event did not resolve.

In the rollover phase, 39 patients (54%) experienced AEs that led to changes in study treatment. There were dose reductions in 22 patients (31%), dose delays in 13 patients (18%), interruptions to study treatment in 10 patients (14%), and permanent discontinuation of study treatment in 10 patients (14%). Related AEs led to permanent discontinuation of study treatment in 7 patients; by patient and severity these were Grade 2 headache, paraesthesia, and flushing, Grade 1 dysgeusia, Grade 2 fatigue, Grade 2 vomiting and nausea, Grade 3 vomiting, Grade 2 nausea, and Grade 3 lymphocyte count decreased (SAE due to requirement for hospitalization); all events except for fatigue resolved.

Overall, gastrointestinal disorders were the main reason leading to changes in study treatment, including dose reductions mainly due to nausea (in 16% of the patients), diarrhea (5%), vomiting (4%), as well as due to fatigue (in 3% of the patients). Additionally, delays or interruptions were mainly due to nausea (in 5% of the patients) and vomiting (4%), as well as due to neutrophil count decreased or neutropenia (4%), and infections such as corona virus infection and influenza like illness (3% each).
