N/A

## 2.6.9. Discussion on clinical safety

The safety profile of resminostat mainly derives from the pivotal study SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN). The other studies evaluating resminostat monotherapy in other malignancies were considered as supportive for safety.

It is acknowledged that safety data from the different studies were not pooled considering the different patient populations (other malignancies), treatment dosage (from 400 to 800mg QD) and regimen (monotherapy and combination) that could affect the characterization of the safety profile of resminostat in the claimed indication.

Despite the small number of patients evaluated in the pivotal phase II study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) in the target population (during the randomized phase resminostat was administered in 100 patients and during rollover phase resminostat was administered in 72 patients) data are available for patients who have been taking the medicinal product for an extended period of time. Considering the rarity of the CTCL, the main safety data set from the randomized and open label phase of the pivotal phase II study in patients with advanced MS or SS with supportive safety data from studies in which resminostat was assessed as monotherapy in other oncological indications could be considered as sufficient in this case. The safety data from the supportive studies are limited by the small number of patients treated at the intended dosage.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The safety assessment is based on the safety data submitted in the initial application, with a data cut-off of 10 March 2023. In the response to D150 LOQ, the applicant presented the overall summary of adverse events from the final safety analysis with the cut-off date of 8 August 2024. Overall, the frequency of TEAE, treatment-related TEAE and serious TEAE is comparable between the 2 analyses with only minor differences. A comprehensive list of all TEAE, SAE, and ADRs (resminostat vs placebo) has been presented and forms the basis for reporting in the SmPC. As of the data cut-off date of 10 March 2023, of the 172 subjects treated with resminostat in RESMAIN study, 57 patients (33.1%) had a duration of exposure of ≥6 months and 31 (18.0%) had a duration of exposure of ≥12 months. Considering the prognosis of the disease, the median OS observed in the pivotal study (i.e. 67.7 months in the resminostat group) and the intended maintenance setting of treatment that could lead to a long treatment exposure (up to 5.3 years in RESMAIN study), the amount of safety data ≥12 months is considered limited with regards to ICH E1 requirements. In the final analysis with the cut-off date of 8 August 2024, data is available for 180 patients treated with resminostat and 100 treated with placebo.

In the safety analysis with data cut-off of 8 August 2024 only 33 patients were followed for &gt; 12 months. A summary of adverse events with start date within 12 months and after 12 months after treatment start is presented comparatively. The higher frequency of events with onset &gt; 12 months (e.g. dose delay and neoplasms) supports the need for long-term safety data. Neoplasms occurred with higher frequency in resminostat treated than in placebo (10 AE occurred in the 12 months from resminostat treatment and 6 additional ones after 12 months from treatment start, while only 4 occurred in the placebo treated patients), however each event (except squamous cell carcinoma) occurred in a single subject and causality assessment is difficult. In fact, according to applicant's assessment of causality, only squamous cell carcinoma was considered related to study drug. In addition, according to literature, CTCL is associated with higher risk of secondary neoplasms.

The risk for carcinogenicity/secondary malignancy is an 'important potential risk' in the safety specification in the RMP to be further characterized in the long-term safety study. On the same note, duration of follow-up beyond 12 months after treatment start remains limited and long-term follow-up is included as 'missing information' in the safety specification of the RMP.

The duration of treatment was comparable across the 2 treatment groups, i.e. mean duration of 287 days (median (range): 127 days (10 - 1939)) and 245 days (median (range): 110 days (14 - 1365)) in resminostat and placebo groups, respectively. The median Relative dose intensity (RDI) was 97.29% in resminostat group and 100% in placebo group suggesting a favourable tolerability of treatment. A wide variation of the RDI of resminostat was however observed, i.e. from 28.0% to 103.7% (vs 71.7% to 103.7% in placebo arm). In the D120 response, the applicant clarified that by number of treatment cycles, 62.2% of patients had a relative dose intensity of 90 to &lt; 110% and only 4.1% had a RDI of &lt; 50% up to 20 treatment cycles. The RDI decreased with the increase number of treatment cycles, potentially indicating a less favorable tolerability of the treatment in long-term setting.

The description below refers to the cut-off date 8-Aug-2024, except for ≥Grade 3 TEAE and treatment related TEAE, which refer to the data cut-off 10-March-2023.

## Adverse events

At the data cut-off of 8-Aug-2024, the incidence of TEAEs was higher in the resminostat group compared to placebo in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, (95.6% vs 82.2% respectively for TEAEs, 37.8% vs 14.9% for Grade ≥3 TEAEs, 23.9% versus 3% for Grade≥3 related TEAEs, 18.3% vs 12.9% for SAEs and 53.3% vs 11.9% for TEAEs leading to changes in study treatment). No Grade 5 AEs were reported in both groups. The AE rates reported in the rollover phase were consistent with the resminostat group of the randomized part. The results of AE analysis in the pivotal and supportive studies were similar. However, it should be noted that the supportive studies were very small.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the adverse events reported with resminostat were consistent with the other HDAC inhibitors. The most frequently reported TEAEs all grades by PT in patients in the resminostat group were nausea (66.7% vs 9.9% in placebo), diarrhoea (42.2% vs 16.8% in placebo), vomiting (32.8% vs 3.0% in placebo), fatigue (33.3% vs 14.9% in placebo), dysgeusia (25.6% vs 2.0% in placebo), muscle spasms (15.6% vs 5.0% in placebo), decreased appetite (20.6% vs 2.0% in placebo), and headache (17.8% vs 9.9% in placebo). PTs reported for &gt; 20% of patients in the rollover phase were similar to the resminostat group of the randomized phase. Also TEAEs reported in the supportive studies with resminostat monotherapy were consistent with RESMAIN study.

The TEAEs reported with resminostat were mainly treatment-related, i.e. 88.0% in resminostat group vs 41.6% in placebo group in the randomized phase, and 88.9% in the rollover phase. The most frequently reported treatment-related TEAEs in the resminostat group vs placebo group in the randomized phase were nausea (68% vs 5.9% respectively), diarrhoea (44.0% vs 8.9%), vomiting (32.0% vs 1.0%), fatigue (29.0% vs 13.9%), dysgeusia (24.0% vs 2.0%), decreased appetite (21% vs 2%)

The majority of Grade ≥ 3 events observed in the resminostat group (data cut-off 10-March-2023) were gastrointestinal disorders reported in 11% of subjects in the randomized phase (0 in placebo), blood disorders including 6% of SOC Blood and lymphatic system (1% in placebo), infection and infestations (6% vs 6% in placebo) and hypertension (5% vs 1% in placebo). In the rollover phase, grade 3 TEAEs were mostly consistent with the randomized phase, i.e. 4% of lymphocyte count decreased, 3% of vomiting, decreased appetite and squamous cell carcinoma, while grade 4 TEAEs were myocardial infarction and supraventricular tachycardia (1% each).

The most represented SOC was gastrointestinal disorders in resminostat group in the randomized phase, i.e. 84.0% versus 36.6% in placebo arm (treatment-related GI disorders: 81.0% vs 19.8%). Most frequent PTs included nausea (69.0% vs 9.9%, treatment-related 68.0% vs 5.9%, and 7% vs 0 grade 3), diarrhoea (50.0% vs 16.8%, treatment-related 44.0% vs 8.9%, and 5% vs 0 grade 3) and vomiting (36.0% vs 3.0%, treatment-related 32.0% vs 1.0%, and 2% vs 0 grade 3). Gastrointestinal disorders in the placebo arm were low grade except for one grade 3 event of pancreatitis. Although no grade 4 events were reported, grade 3 events represented about 10% of the events. The severe cases of gastrointestinal disorders are reflected in the description of ADR in section 4.8 of the proposed SmPC.

Gastrointestinal toxicity is a class effect of HDAC inhibitors, and the majority of these events were assessed as treatment-related.

Gastrointestinal side effects nausea, diarrhoea, and vomiting occurred in a large number of patients and typically started within the 1 st or 2 nd treatment cycle, on treatment days and resolved during the offtreatment days; median duration of event was 3-5 days. Most of these events have resolved without treatment modifications (84% of vomiting, 89% of nausea, 95% of diarrhea), however few events led to dose reduction and treatment discontinuation.

About 33% of events of vomiting, 61% of nausea, and 27% of diarrhea required treatment with anti-emetics (metoclopramide, aprepitant and prochlorperazine) and loperamide.

Specific recommendations for management of gastrointestinal side effects nausea, vomiting, and diarrhoea are to be given in section 4.2 and 4.4 of the proposed SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Myelosuppression is considered a class effect of HDAC inhibitors which may lead to an increased risk for infections and bleeding events. Haematological toxicity as observed under the SOC Blood and lymphatic system disorders, were more frequently reported with resminostat (18.3%) than placebo (11.9%) in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study (treatment-related 14.0% vs 5.9%). PT under this SOC included neutropenia (5.0% in resminostat group vs 0 in placebo group), anaemia (5.6% vs 4%), thrombocytopenia (3.9% vs 1%), leukopenia (1.7% vs 0), lymphopenia (1.7%vs 2%), monocytosis (2.2% vs 0), lymphadenopathy (2.2% vs 4%)

Haematological events represented 31% of reported AE. Of these, 23% were Grade 3 and 1% Grade 4. Grades ≥3 events were reported for PTs of neutropenia (6% vs 0), lymphopenia (1% in both groups) and lymphadenopathy (1% vs 0). Events of grade 4 in the resminostat group were neutrophil count decreased and lymphocyte count decreased (1% each). A grade 4 lymphopenia occurred in placebo group. Although all thrombocytopenia observed were grade 1, one case of fatal tumour haemorraghe, concomitant with a thrombocytopenia has been reported in a supportive study.

It is noted that a Grade 3 lymphocyte count decreased (SAE due to requirement for hospitalization) occurring in the rollover phase led to treatment discontinuation and resolved.

93% of haematological events resolved, 83% without dose modifications, and 96% did not require treatment. 2 events of anaemia grade 2 and 1 event of neutrophil count decreased required treatment (RBC transfusion and respectively GCSF).

Neutropenia and thrombocytopenia are to be described in section 4.8. and recommendations for monitoring and dose modifications for events of thrombocytopenia and neutropenia are to be given in section 4.2 and 4.4 of the SmPC. The need for at least monthly haematological monitoring is indicated in the proposed SmPC.

Lymphopenia or lymphocyte count decreased were reported for both treatment groups in the RESMAIN study with similar frequency (5.8% of patients in the resminostat group vs 5.0% in the placebo group). Considering the myelosuppressive effect of resminostat, the Grade 3/4 lymphocyte count decreased with resminostat versus placebo (3.7% vs 2.0%), and a related Grade 3 lymphopenia SAE in the rollover phase, the term 'lymphopenia' was added to the Tabulated list of adverse reactions. In addition, infections due to neutropenia are to be described in the SmPC and included as 'important potential risk' in the RMP.

Vascular disorders were reported 2 times more in resminostat than placebo group, i.e. 18.9% vs 9.9%, respectively. Hypertension was the vascular TEAE the most reported with resminostat (7.2% in resminostat and 7.9% in placebo groupand hot flush (2.2% vs 0 in resminostat and placebo groups, respectively). One case of serious deep vein thrombosis reported in resminostat group was considered as treatment-related.

Cardiac disorders were more frequently reported in resminostat than in placebo group in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, i.e. 10% vs 5% (treatment-related 5% vs 1%). In the rollover phase, 9.7% of subjects experienced a cardiac disorder (treatment-related 2.8%). The most reported cardiac TEAEs with resminostat was palpitations (3.9 vs 0 in placebo group and 2.8% in the randomized phase and rollover phase, respectively). Severe and serious cardiac TEAEs were reported with resminostat, i.e. one Grade 3 myocardial ischaemia in resminostat group (treatment-related), one Grade 3 myocardial ischaemia in the rollover phase (SAE), one Grade 4 myocardial infarction in the rollover phase (SAE), one Grade 4 supraventricular tachycardia in the rollover phase (SAE). Myocardial ischaemia and blood creatinine phosphokinase B increased are to be included in section 4.8 of the proposed SmpC and cardiac disease (Myocardial infarction, myocardial ischaemia, and supraventricular tachycardia) are to be included in the description of selected adverse drug reactions in the SmPC. In addition, cases of tachycardia, arrhythmia, sinus tachycardia, atrial fibrillation, ventricular extrasystole were reported in subjects treated with resminostat 600mg in the supportive studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Extensive cardiac monitoring was performed during all the clinical studies. Overall, the data suggest that resminostat does not prolong QT. Changes in T wave morphology and ST segment depression, which have been reported with HDAC inhibitors, were noted, but patients did not develop severe ventricular arrhythmias. QT prolongation is considered an 'important potential risk' in the RMP. It is mentioned in the proposed SmPC that patients with uncontrolled or significant cardiac disease (e.g., unstable angina pectoris, uncontrolled hypertension, congestive heart failure (NYHA Class III or IV) related to primary cardiac disease, a condition requiring anti arrhythmic therapy, ischemic or severe valvular heart disease, or a myocardial infarction in the last 6 months) have not been studied and should be monitored.

## AEs of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events (data cut-off 10-Mar2023)

Considering the mechanism of action of resminostat, the nonclinical findings and the safety profiles of other HDAC inhibitors and their potential class effects, the following were identified as AESI: myelosuppression, (including infections and bleeding events), QT prolongation and arrhythmia, thromboembolic events, second primary malignancy, hepatotoxicity (increased ALT/AST) and acute renal failure.

SAEs and treatment-related SAEs occurred at a higher rate in patients in resminostat group compared to placebo group in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, i.e. 19% vs 11.9% and 11.0% vs 1.0%, respectively. No TEAE leading to death was reported in RESMAIN study.

In RESMAIN study, the SAEs by SOC that were more reported with resminostat than placebo were infections (8% vs 5%, incl. cellulitis 2% vs 0 and pneumonia 2% vs 0), gastrointestinal disorders (5% vs 0, incl. diarrhoea 2% vs 0) and respiratory disorders (4% vs 0). The majority of SAEs considered as related to resminostat across all studies belong to the SOC gastrointestinal disorders (4% vs 0). One treatment-related SAE of pulmonary embolism and one treatment-related SAE of deep vein thrombosis occurred in resminostat group and are supportive of a risk of thromboembolic events within resminostat and the warning included in section 4.4 of the SmPC is supported. Other SAEs of interest due to the drug class included Alanine aminotransferase increased (1 patient in the randomized part and 1 patient in the roll over part), Aspartate aminotransferase increased (1 patient) and acute kidney injury (1 patient in the randomized part).

No treatment-related deaths were reported during the randomised phase of pivotal study or any of the supportive studies. Until data cut-off in Study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) (10 March 2023), 28 patients in the resminostat group and 29 patients in the placebo group, including those subsequently treated with resminostat in the rollover phase, had died. In the response to D120 LOQ, the applicant provided information on the seven deaths cases due to TEAEs reported with resminostat monotherapy and in combination across all clinical studies. Only 2 cases were considered possibly or probably related to resminostat: one case of liver failure/renal failure was considered unlikely related to resminostat or sorafenib and one case of tumour haemorrhage induced shock haemorrhagic was considered probably related to resminostat.

The majority of patients in both groups of pivotal study RESMAIN had MF Stage IIB disease (59 patients in resminostat group, 58 patients in placebo group), however no significant differences in treatment related adverse events have been observed in MF Stage IIB disease versus later stage MF and SS. The frequency of SAE (33.3% vs 15.5%) and hospitalizations (30.0 vs 14.8%) was higher in Sezary Syndrome than the Mycosis Fungoides disease, notably in the SOC 'Infections and infestations' however of same order of magnitude for SmPC reporting (common).

Discontinuations (data cut-off 10-Mar-2023)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall the AEs leading to changes in trial treatment were more frequent in resminostat than placebo group in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, i.e. 51.0% vs 11.9%. These included dose reductions, dose delays, treatment interruptions, and permanent discontinuation of resminostat 600 mg. GI events led to most cases of dose modification (reduction or interruption).

The serious TEAE myocardial infarction and myocardial ischaemia chest pain, lymphopenia, and pulmonary embolism (1 case each) led to treatment discontinuation. Diarrhoea (2 cases), cellulitis, ALT/AST increased, decreased appetite, diabetes mellitus, and acute kidney injury (1 case each) led to dose modifications. One event of myocardial infarction and one of blood creatinine phosphokinase increased were grade 4.

The TEAEs leading to treatment discontinuation were reported in 8.0% of subjects from resminostat group and 3.0% of subjects in placebo group in the randomized part of RESMAIN study and in 14% of patients in the rollover phase. In addition to the higher rate of treatment discontinuation due to a TEAE in resminostat group compared to placebo group, it is noticed an unusual higher rate of discontinuation due to patient withdrawal in resminostat compared to placebo (i.e. 25.0% vs 5.0%, refer to participant flow in efficacy section) that cannot exclude a safety reason, suggesting an unfavorable safety profile of resminostat. The most reported related TEAEs by SOC leading to resminostat discontinuation was gastrointestinal disorders including low grade events (Grade 1 nausea and vomiting, Grade 2 nausea and vomiting, Grade 3 vomiting).

Related AEs led to dose reductions in almost all patients who had dose reductions (31%) and to dose delays and treatment interruptions in 16% of patients, each. These included serious diarrhea, acute kidney injury, decreased appetite, diabetes mellitus, and cellulitis (dose reductions in five patients) and serious upper abdominal pain, transaminase increases, decreased appetite, and cellulitis (dose delays in three patients). Seven patients (7%) in the resminostat group experienced related AEs of Grade 1 - 3 severity that led to permanent discontinuation of treatment (vomiting and nausea (two patients each) and weight decreased, dermatitis allergic, myocardial ischaemia, pulmonary embolism (SAE), and fatigue (one patient each)).

The majority of TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation of resminostat resolved. However, in addition to several unresolved cases, time to resolution was quite long for a few events (e.g. fatigue, myocardial necrosis marker increased, neutropenia). It appears that persistence of AE of low grade led to treatment discontinuation (consent withdrawn, physician decision), while multiple or prolonged dose modifications (reduction, delay) during the treatment course resulted possibly in disease progression.

In the rollover phase of the pivotal study (RESMAIN)], 54% of patients experienced AEs that led to changes in study treatment. There were dose reductions in 22 patients (31%), dose delays in 13 patients (18%), treatment interruptions in 10 patients (14%), and permanent discontinuation of study treatment in 10 patients (14%). Seven patients experienced related AEs of Grade 1 - 3 severity that led to permanent discontinuation of study treatment (vomiting and nausea (2 patients each)), and headache, paraesthesia, flushing, dysgeusia, fatigue, and lymphocyte count decreased (SAE) (1 patient each)).

Resminostat exposure data with cut-off 08-Aug-2024 for patients who had dose reduction, dose interruption or delay compared to the patients who did not have these dose modifications are provided. Based on data on dose intensity, total cumulative dose, it seems that in the randomised and rollover phase of this study treatment modifications did not interfere with administration of resminostat.

## Special populations

In terms of age, 58 of 172 patients (33.7%) treated with resminostat had an age of ≥ 65 to 74 years and 25 of 172 patients (14.5.0%) were 75 years or older, including 1 patient older than 85 years. The highest incidence of TEAE is noted in the 65-75 years range. Patients above 65 years of age had higher incidence of SAE, hospitalization, blood and lymphatic disorders (mainly thrombocytopenia) diarrhea, and thromboembolic events, supporting the proposed recommendation to monitor patients&gt; 65 years closely for gastrointestinal and haematological toxicity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No dedicated studies were performed in patients with hepatic impairment and this is proposed to be reflected in the SmPC.

Regarding renal impairment, 68 of 172 patients (39.5%) treated with resminostat had mild renal impairment and 19 of 172 patients (11.0%) had moderate renal impairment. In patients with mild renal impairment, frequency and severity of AEs were similar compared to patients with no renal impairment. In patients with moderate renal impairment, diarrhea, vomiting and thrombocytopenia were more frequently reported than in no/mild impairment patients; however the small sample size does not allow to draw any conclusion.

Based on non-clinical data, resminostat is expected to be teratogenic due to its mechanism of action. Genotoxicity of resminostat cannot be excluded based on in vitro and in vivo data. There are no data from the use of resminostat in pregnant women. Therefore, in view of the molecule's profile and, in particular, its mechanism of action, pregnancy and breastfeeding should be contraindicated during treatment. A contraception should be used during treatment and for at least six months after the last dose in women of childbearing potential and for at least three months after the last dose in sexually active men. With regard to fertility and the ability to conceive, although there are no dedicated non clinical study (ICH S9), effects on male and female fertility can be expected considering the findings on male and female reproductive organs observed without significant safety margins in repeat dose toxicity studies in both mice and dogs. These effects are not limited to high doses. In addition, studies in dogs (beagles) have shown a lack of reversibility in sperm parameters (amount and quality of ejaculate decreased).

## Drug interactions

According to the Applicant, electrocardiogram QT prolongation was an important potential risk of Kinselby. Nonetheless, considering the limitations of the interpretation of the safety results regarding the QT prolongation, a conservative wording regarding the concomitant use of medicinal products known to prolong the QT interval is to be included in the SmPC.

## Laboratory findings

In the randomized phase of the pivotal study RESMAIN, most changes in laboratory parameters were of Grade 0 - 2 severity.

Haematology abnormalities were more observed in resminostat group than placebo group in the randomized phase, i.e. leukocytes (51% vs 19%), lymphocytes (72% vs 55%), neutrophils (42% vs 8%), platelets (58% vs 6%), reflecting the known class effect of HDAC inhibitors of myelosuppression.

Abnormal laboratory parameters considered clinically significant in the opinion of the investigator were reported as AE under the SOCs 'Blood and lymphatic system disorders', 'Investigations', and 'Metabolism and nutrition disorders'. There were more grade 3 neutrophils decreased in resminostat arm than in placebo arm (11% vs 0). Leukocytes decreased and platelets decreased that were more frequent in resminostat arm than placebo arm were mostly grade 1 and 2.

Regarding clinical chemistry, treatment related grade 1 ALT increased, bilirubin increased, and calcium decreased occurred more frequently in resminostat arm (31%, 14%, and 28% respectively) than in placebo arm (25%, 5%, and 20%). In addition, grade 2 creatine kinase, creatinine, and glucose were higher in resminostat arm (12%, 22%, and 27% respectively) than in placebo arm (6%, 2%, and 15% respectively).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Cases of increased glucose, potassium, calcium, and sodium are described, however none are considered adverse reactions of resminostat.

Considering the prominent gastrointestinal toxicity reported with resminostat (mainly nausea, diarrhoea and vomiting), electrolyte imbalances were expected; however no clear difference between the two groups was observed. The proposed wording regarding the monitoring of fluid and electrolyte blood levels in section 4.4 is agreed at the present time.

No summary of vital signs was provided by the Applicant. Based on safety data from the RESMAIN study, it is noticed a more important change from baseline of mean and median weight in resminostat compared to placebo group, as expected considering the gastrointestinal toxicity. The PT weight decreased is to be reported as an ADR of resminostat in section 4.8 of the SmPC.

## 2.6.10. Conclusions on the clinical safety

The overall safety profile of resminostat is consistent with other HDAC inhibitors including a prominent gastrointestinal toxicity (mainly nausea, diarrhoea, vomiting), myelosuppression (anemia, lymphopenia, neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia) and cases of thromboembolic events including serious AEs. Frequent side-effects of resminostat (especially gastro-intestinal side-effects, cytopenias) can impact the patient's quality of life and long-term treatment compliance, which is a concern in the maintenance treatment setting. The non-negligible rates of grade ≥3 TEAEs, discontinuation of treatment due to TEAEs and due to patient withdrawal also raise concerns on its tolerability as long-term treatment. The frequency of neoplasms is considerably higher in the resminostat treated than in placebo treated, and although the majority of these events are not considered related to resminostat, this remains a significant uncertainty. To this end, lack of long-term (&gt; 12 months) safety data for a larger dataset is a major limitation of this submission. The safety profile of resminostat in patients with renal or hepatic impairment was not studied.

## 3. Benefit-Risk Balance

## 3.1. Therapeutic Context

## 3.1.1. Disease or condition

Kinselby is proposed to be indicated for treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS) that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy or Total Skin Electron Beam therapy (TSEB).

The recommended dose of resminostat is 600 mg per day taken orally once daily. Treatment is to be administered on days 1 - 5 of a 14-day treatment cycle; the 5 days of treatment are followed by a 9-day treatment-free period. Treatment is to continue until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

## 3.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

At present, there is no curative treatment available for patients with CTCL except for allogenic stem cell transplantation which is suitable to a limited number of patients.

Patients with early-stage disease (Stages IA, IB and IIA) are generally treated with skin-directed therapies (SDTs) such as topical steroids, psoralen photochemotherapy (psoralen + ultraviolet A light (UVA), PUVA), narrow band ultraviolet B light (UVB), localized and whole skin radiotherapy, and topical cytostatic agents such as chlormethine gel (mechlorethamine).

For advanced-stage MF (IIB, III and IV) and SS, systemic therapies are the treatment of choice. The most frequently used or recommended therapies include retinoids (e.g., bexarotene), interferons, HDAC inhibitors (not approved in the European Union (EU)), brentuximab vedotin, the anti-CCR4 monoclonal antibody mogamulizumab, extracorporeal photopheresis (ECP; for erythrodermic MF and SS), and chemotherapy.

Table 2 presents the treatment recommendations for MF and SS based on EORTC guidelines.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Source: Willemze et al., 2018

Currently available treatment approaches are very heterogenous and rarely lead to durable remissions and eventually become less effective, requiring multiple therapy changes for the patients. Treatment response rates are typical between 30-40% and response duration tend to be less than 1 year underlining the unmet medical need for improved therapies maintaining durable response and offering improved quality of life.

## 3.1.3. Main clinical studies

One pivotal study 4SC-201-6-2015 (RESMAIN) was submitted. RESMAIN was a phase II multicenter, doubleblind, randomized, placebo-controlled study which assessed the efficacy, and safety of resminostat monotherapy versus placebo in patients with advanced-stage CTCL (MF or SS) that had achieved disease control (SD, PR and CR) with previous systemic therapy.

The primary endpoint was progression-free survival (PFS). Secondary endpoints were TTP, TTNT, PFS2, PFS3, OS, and changes in HrQoL parameters including time to symptom worsening (TTSW) in terms of pruritus as a key secondary endpoint, and other additional secondary QoL parameters assessed through VAS, Skindex-29, and FACT-G questionnaires.

## 3.2. Favourable effects

The main analysis showed a statistically significant improvement in PFS for resminostat compared with placebo, with a median PFS of 8.3 months on resminostat and 4.2 months on placebo, and a HR of 0.623 (95%CI: XXXX; p = 0.015). The provided sensitivity analyses showed a consistent and significant PFS

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

difference in favor of resminostat, except the sensitivity analysis, where discontinuation due to toxicity or the start of a new treatment before PD were considered as an event (PD), however, the PFS result was numerically in favour of resminostat.

Consistent improvements in TTP (HR 0.623; 95% CI [0.424, 0.916] median TTP: 8.3 vs 4.2 months) and TTNT (HR 0.594; median TTNT: 8.8 vs 4.2 months) were reported.

Subgroup analyses of PFS revealed HRs in favor of resminostat (particularly for patients with bexarotene and other retinoids as last systemic therapy prior to study entry, patients with MF, patients with SD and patients with stage IVA2 - IVB disease at baseline and patients with LCT) except for TSEB or ECP therapy as last prior systemic therapy. Subgroup analyses for patients with SS and male patients have shown poorer PFS improvement with treatment.

Post-hoc mSWAT MNAR analysis of the observed changes from baseline over time (i.e., Day 29 to Day 1,093) for resminostat versus placebo were nominally statistically significant in favor of resminostat (p-value: 0.0199).

In line with these findings, time to development or worsening of skin tumours (TTTE) was delayed with resminostat (44.2 months 95% CI: 23.1, NE) compared with placebo (6.5 months; 95% CI: 3.7, NE) with a HR of 0.333.

Data do not indicate a statistically significant difference between treatment arms for PFS2 (p-value 0.156, HR 0.758 (0.516, 1.113)) and there is no difference in PFS3 between treatment arms (p-value 0.921, HR 1.021 (0.674, 1.547)). However, the KM curve suggests a slight trend for a longer PFS2 in patients in the resminostat arm compared to placebo. The results for OS at the final analysis were similar to those at the main analysis and showed no significant difference between the treatment arms (p-value 0.940, HR 0.983 (0.606, 1.590)).

## 3.3. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

The placebo as a comparator for patients with SD or PR is questioned. In clinical practice, many treatments could be offered as long as the patient did not experience complete response among the range of treatments for advanced MF and SS, notably at initial therapy or after the use of a few systemic therapies.

The population enrolled is too heterogeneous in terms of number of prior systemic therapy received (1-21 lines) without stratification.

Due to the gastrointestinal side effects associated with resminostat, the efficacy of blinding was questioned during scientific advice (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/33911/2016), and a survey was conducted to collect the patients and investigators' assumptions regarding the assigned treatment arm, prior to unblinding. The large proportion of patients correctly assuming their treatment in both arms likely reflects an important level of functional unblinding, which may have introduced bias in several efficacy analyses (including the analysis of primary and secondary endpoints).

The censoring rules defined for PFS are not in line with EMA guidance EMA/CHMP/27994/2008/Rev.1. Indeed, a treatment policy strategy would have been preferred for the primary analysis, i.e. with no censoring applied prior to non-study cancer treatment nor in case of at least two consecutive incomplete / missing GRAs. As requested, the Applicant provided an alternative PFS analysis where censoring rules were modified. However, the absence of GRS scheduled assessments after treatment discontinuation prevents any meaningful interpretation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Functional unblinding may have introduced bias in PFS analyses, considering that the skin, nodes and viscera components of the global response score used to derive PFS were investigator-assessed. The Applicant clarified that an independent central review of mSWAT assessment is not feasible based on photographs. Indeed, it would often be impossible to differentiate between patches and plaques, or skin areas with inflammatory changes from erythhrodermic skin, based on photographs only. Skin being the leading disease compartment with 82% of disease events occurring in skin, a potential central review of other components (LN and viscera) would not substantially change the reliability of the primary endpoint. The impossibility to perform a blinded central review prevents a meaningful assessment of the potential bias introduced by functional unblinding.

The stability of study results was questioned based on the sensitivity analysis where discontinuation due to toxicity or start of new treatment before PD were considered as an event. Indeed, this sensitivity analysis showed a smaller effect size and did not reach nominal statistical significance. Given the safety profile of resminostat and the imbalance observed in treatment withdrawals for toxicity, it may be reasonable to consider discontinuations due to toxicity or new treatments before PD as unfavourable outcomes - and therefore a sensible assumption to consider them as events in this alternative PFS analysis.

It was confirmed that the vast majority of the PFS censoring was non-administrative and indicates informative censoring. As suspected, it is also apparent that withdrawals due to tolerability issues or drugrelated adverse events were the main non-administrative reason for PFS censoring in the resminostat treatment group. This pattern was not observed in the placebo arm. Instead, several censored measurements in the placebo arm (more than in the resminostat arm) were due to physician decisions based on increases in mSWAT not fulfilling PD criteria, and a few others due to treatment withdrawal for lack of efficacy / patient's assumption to be on placebo.

A post-censoring hazard higher in the resminostat arm than in the placebo arm for non-administratively censored patients is a plausible assumption. In this context, the results of the tipping point question the statistical robustness. Indeed, most investigated scenarios with higher multiplicative increases in the resminostat arm than in the placebo arm result in loss of statistical significance, with smaller effect sizes than in the primary analysis. For instance, increasing the post-censoring hazard for the resmonistat group by only a factor of 1.25 leads to the loss of significance.

Results of TTP and TTNT are of exploratory nature given the absence of adjustment for multiplicity. The Applicant clarified that 72 (84.7%) of TTNT events in the placebo arm correspond to patients crossing over to resminostat. This confirms that TTNT is very likely biased. Indeed, crossover can only be an option for patients in the placebo arm. Whether as many patients would have initiated a subsequent therapy in the absence of crossover, and whether these subsequent therapies would have been initiated at the same time point, is speculative and unlikely.

It is noted that some emphasis is given to the post-hoc analysis of PFS from start of last prior systemic therapy to first PD. However, the use of last prior systemic therapy as a starting point, instead of randomisation, is subject to immortal time bias which has not been accounted for. This has likely resulted in a distortion of the observed treatment effect.

PFS2, PFS3 and OS are of exploratory nature due to the absence of adjustment for multiplicity. Importantly, given the large confidence limits, the OS data cannot rule out a substantial detriment on survival. PFS3 analysis showed no difference between treatment arms. Analyses were performed by the Applicant to investigate the effect of crossover on OS results. These analyses did not provide reassurance on the OS data.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In fact, analyses adjusting for crossover consistently showed no treatment effect on OS, and even indicated a potential detriment.

In both treatment arms, the key secondary endpoint TTSW pruritus was not evaluable due to the low number of events in the TTSW and ITT population. The difference in TTSW was not statistically significant between the treatment arms (HR 1.215 [0.538, 2.742]).

There was no difference in the observed changes from baseline for resminostat and placebo in the VAS itching and skindex-29 questionnaire. The observed changes from baseline over time for resminostat vs placebo in the FACT-G questionnaire were in favor of placebo (notably functional and physical well-being subscales), even when the modified FACT-G total score (without counting \"nausea\"), was used. In line with the results in VAS itching, the use of anti-itching medication was similar between the two treatment arms.

The difference in median PFS between the treatment arms for the subgroup of SS patients was smaller (4.2 months for placebo vs 5.6 months for resminostat) than for the subgroup of MF patients (4.2 months for placebo vs 9.2 months for resminostat) due mainly to the lower mPFS reported in the resminostat arm for SS group. The imbalance in disease characteristics and prognostic factors could possibly explain the reported limited results in PFS. However, it cannot be excluded that these results may as well translate an absence of a clinical effect in SS a different entity of CTCL than MF with more aggressive disease course. It is than difficult or even impossible to assess the extent of efficacy of resminostat in SS and there is no retrospective solution to this issue.

Patients in the resminostat arm received slightly more corticosteroids than patients in the placebo arm (51 vs. 45.5%), more antihistamines (45 vs 42.6%), and more systemic corticosteroids (24 vs 14.9%). This could reflect a lack of relevant efficacy.

## 3.4. Unfavourable effects

The incidence of TEAEs was higher in the resminostat group compared to placebo in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, (95.6% vs 82.2% respectively for TEAEs, 37.8% vs 14.9% for Grade ≥3 TEAEs, 23.9% versus 3% for Grade≥3 related TEAEs, 18.3% vs 12.9% for SAEs and 53.3% vs 11.9% for TEAEs leading to changes in study treatment). No Grade 5 AEs were reported in both groups. The AE rates reported in the rollover phase were consistent with the resminostat group of the randomized part. The results of adverse events (AE) analysis in the pivotal and supportive studies were similar. However, it should be noted that the supportive studies were very small.

The safety profile of resminostat is mainly driven by gastrointestinal toxicity. Indeed, 81.1% of patients experienced an event within the SOC gastrointestinal disorders. Most frequent PTs included nausea (66.7% vs 9.9), diarrhoea (42.2% vs 16.8) and vomiting (32.8% vs 3.0). Although no grade 4 events were reported, grade 3 events represented about 10% of the events, and 5% of the patients who experienced a SAE were related to gastro-intestinal toxicity. Gastrointestinal events were the main reason for treatment dose modification (16% of patients). Overall, the ADRs related to GI toxicities and the severe cases of gastrointestinal disorders are reflected in the description of ADR in section 4.8 of the proposed SmPC. Management of GI events is addressed in the proposed SmpC.

Haematological toxicity as observed under the SOC Blood and lymphatic system disorders were more frequently reported with resminostat (18.3%) than placebo (11.9%) in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study. PTs under this SOC were neutropenia (5.0% in resminostat vs 0 in placebo), anaemia (5.6% vs 4%), thrombocytopenia (3.9% vs 1%), leukopenia (1.7% vs 0), lymphopenia (1.7% vs 2%), monocytosis (2.2%

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

vs 0), lymphadenopathy (2.2% vs 4%). Additional events were reported within the SOC investigations. Grades ≥3 events were reported for PTs of neutropenia (4% vs 0) lymphopenia (1% in both groups) and lymphadenopathy (1% vs 0). Grade 4 events (neutropenia and lymphopenia, 1 each) were reported. Management of haematological events is described in the proposed SmPC.

Furthermore, vascular disorders were reported 2-times more in resminostat than placebo group, i.e. 18.9% vs 9.9%, respectively. Hypertension was the vascular TEAE the most reported with resminostat (7.2% in resminostat and 7.9% in placebo group) and hot flush (2.2% vs 0 in resminostat and placebo groups, respectively). One case of serious deep vein thrombosis reported in resminostat group was considered as treatment-related and included as ADR in section 4.8 of the proposed SmPC.

Finally, cardiac disorders were more frequently reported in resminostat than in placebo group in the randomized phase of RESMAIN study, i.e. 10% vs 5% (treatment-related 5% vs 1%). In the rollover phase, 9.7% of subjects experienced a cardiac disorder (treatment-related 2.8%). The most reported cardiac TEAEs with resminostat was palpitations (3.9% vs 0 in placebo group and 2.8% in the randomized phase and rollover phase, respectively). Severe and serious cardiac TEAEs were reported with resminostat, i.e. one Grade 3 myocardial ischaemia in resminostat group (treatment-related), one Grade 3 myocardial ischaemia in the rollover phase (SAE), one Grade 4 myocardial infarction in the rollover phase (SAE), one Grade 4 supraventricular tachycardia in the rollover phase (SAE). In addition, cases of tachycardia, arrhythmia, sinus tachycardia, atrial fibrillation, ventricular extrasystole were reported in subjects treated with resminostat 600mg in the supportive studies. Electrocardiogram QT prolonged is a known ADR of panobinostat (Farydak) and, conservatively, wording is included in the proposed SmPC with regards to the possibility of coprescription of drugs susceptible to prolong the QTc in these indications and the predisposition of certain patients.

The frequency of neoplasms is considerably higher in the treatment group than in placebo (16 vs 4 cases). The majority of these events are observed in one single subject each and are not considered related to resminostat. Squamous cell carcinoma of the skin is considered treatment related.

## 3.5. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

Frequent side-effects (especially gastro-intestinal side-effects, cytopenias) of resminostat can impact the patient's quality of life, treatment tolerability, and long-term treatment compliance with potential impact on the treatment efficacy

The extent of safety database does not fulfil the requirement of ICH E1 for long term safety with only 3 patients who received resminostat 600 mg for a duration &gt; 12 months.

An unusual higher rate of discontinuation due to patient withdrawal in resminostat compared to placebo was observed, which cannot exclude a safety reason.

A considerably higher rate of neoplasms occurred in the resminostat treated subjects compared to placebo. The majority occurred in a single subject each, and causality assessment is difficult.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.7. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 3.7.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The efficacy of resminostat was assessed in one single pivotal randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase II study. Primary endpoint was met and was statistically significant, however, the observed PFS effect is modest, and its robustness is questioned due to methodological issues, i.e., the extent of informative censoring, the large imbalance in withdrawals due to toxicity and the potential impact of functional unblinding. Furthermore, the PFS finding is not supported by clinically relevant secondary endpoints, in particular TTSW and QoL, thus significantly limiting interpretation of resminostat clinical benefit particularly for SS subtype associated with poorer prognosis and for which a limited mPFS gain of 1.4 months was reported in the study. Moreover, there is no treatment difference in OS, and the absence of treatment effect persists after adjustment for crossover (with sensitivity analyses indicating a potential detriment).

Overall, due to high frequency of adverse events (38 % of ≥ grade 3 events, 18 % of SAE and with 53 % of patients experiencing an AE that led to modification in study treatment, of which 10% of discontinuation), the safety profile of resminostat appears burdensome with regards to the sought indication of maintenance treatment in the context of the uncertainties discussed. The main toxicities are related to gastrointestinal events and neutropenia, the proposed management of which would have been addressed in the SmPC. In addition, there is also an uncertainty on the notable higher discontinuation due to patient withdrawal and respectively higher frequency of neoplasms in resminostat arm vs placebo. In view of the uncertainties in relation to the efficacy the added burden of toxicities is of concern.

## 3.7.2. Balance of benefits and risks

There are important limitations and uncertainties preventing the conclusion on the clinical benefit of resminostat in patients with advanced stage MF and SS that have achieved at least SD with at least one prior systemic therapy. These are mainly related to concerns on the translation of PFS gain into clinical benefit, and the statistical/methodological robustness of results. Although the overall safety profile of resminostat is consistent with the other HDAC inhibitors, and mainly related to gastrointestinal toxicity, the non-negligible rates of grade ≥3 TEAEs, discontinuation of treatment due to TEAEs and to patient withdrawal, and relatively high rate of neoplasms and raise concerns on its tolerability as long-term treatment, would be a substantial burden to the patient in the absence of a clinical relevant benefit.

## 3.7.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

N/A

## 3.8. Conclusions

The overall benefit/risk balance of Kinselby is negative.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4. Recommendations

## Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

The CHMP by consensus is of the opinion that Kinselby is not similar to Adcetris, Ledaga, and Porteligeo within the meaning of Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No. 847/2000. See Appendix on Similarity.

## Outcome

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy for Kinselby in the treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides (MF) and Sézary syndrome (SS) that have achieved at least stable disease with at least one prior systemic therapy or Total Skin Electron Beam therapy (TSEB), the CHMP considers by consensus that

[a] the efficacy of the above-mentioned medicinal product is not sufficiently demonstrated,

and therefore, recommends the refusal of the granting of the marketing authorisation for the abovementioned medicinal product. The CHMP considers that:

- The robustness of the PFS analysis could not be shown in the single pivotal trial aimed at investigating the maintenance treatment of patients with advanced stage mycosis fungoides and Sézary syndrome due to methodological weaknesses. This is due to the extent of informative censoring, the increased treatment discontinuation rate in the resminostat arm for toxicity reasons, and the potential impact of functional unblinding. The assessment of PFS is complicated by the absence of consistent global response assessments after patients discontinuing from blinded study treatment.
- In addition to this, no effect on PFS2 could be demonstrated in this study of maintenance treatment. No treatment difference could be observed for overall survival (OS), with large confidence limits reflecting considerable uncertainty. No clear improvement was seen in patient reported outcomes. In this context, the substantial adverse event burden of treatment is noted.

Due to the aforementioned concerns a satisfactory summary of product characteristics, labelling, package leaflet, pharmacovigilance system, risk management plan and post-authorisation measures to address other concerns as outlined in the list of outstanding issues cannot be agreed at this stage.

Furthermore, following review of the available data in the context of the applicant's claim of new active substance status, the CHMP position at the time of this report is reflected in Appendix 2. However, in light of the negative recommendation, the CHMP is of the opinion that it is not appropriate to conclude on the new active substance status at this time.
